--- Page 1 ---
<meta>This page represents the front cover and binding of the manuscript, providing the first indication of its royal or aristocratic provenance.</meta>

<note>The front cover is bound in aged vellum—a durable, high-quality parchment made from animal skin—which has developed a characteristic yellowed patina, staining, and small spots over the centuries. In the center of the cover is a prominent gold-stamped heraldic emblem known as a <term>supralibros</term> (original: Latin for "upon the books"), which serves as a decorative mark of ownership. 

This specific emblem identifies the book as part of a royal collection, likely belonging to Augustus II the Strong or Augustus III, Kings of Poland and Electors of Saxony. The emblem features two shields side-by-side under a single magnificent crown, symbolizing a personal union between two states. The shield on the left displays the arms of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth: the White Eagle of Poland and the <term>Pahonia</term> (a charging knight) representing the Grand Duchy of Lithuania. The shield on the right displays the arms of the Electorate of Saxony, marked by horizontal bars and a diagonal <term>crancelin</term> (a stylized crown of rue). A smaller shield at the very center typically would contain the monarch's personal royal monogram.

On the left edge of the cover, small remnants of leather or fabric ties are visible; these were once used to pull the heavy covers tight to prevent the parchment pages from warping. At the top edge, there are <unclear>faint, illegible handwritten marks</unclear> likely added by a later librarian or collector.</note>

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript, bound in vellum and featuring the gold-stamped heraldic arms of the Polish-Saxon union, indicating royal provenance.</summary>

<keywords>binding, vellum, supralibros, heraldry, Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, Saxony, Augustus II, Augustus III, provenance</keywords>

<meta>This is the formal title page of the work identified on the previous page. It confirms the authorship of Francis Bacon and places this specific treatise within his broader project to reform the sciences.</meta>

->FRANCIS<-
->BARON<-
->OF<-
->VERULAM,<-
->VISCOUNT<-
->OF ST. ALBANS,<-

# History of Life and Death.
->OR,<-

## THE SECOND TITLE
## in the Natural and Experimental History
## for the building up of Philosophy:

### Which is
### THE THIRD PART
### OF THE GREAT INSTAURATION.
<note>original: "Instaurationis Magnæ"; this refers to Bacon's "Great Instauration" or "Great Renewal," his ambitious, multi-volume plan to overhaul all human knowledge and scientific methodology.</note>

->In the Workshop of John Haviland, at the expense of<-
->MATTHEW LOWNES, 1623.<-

<note>Bacon’s "Six Monthly Titles" (Sex Designationes Menstruas) was his ambitious plan to publish one volume of natural history every month. --- Page 11 ---
<meta>While the previous page featured a transcription from John Calvin’s "Institutes," this page begins a new section from a different work: Francis Bacon’s "Historia Vitae et Mortis" (History of Life and Death), published in 1623. This work is part of his larger project, the "Instauratio Magna," where he applies his inductive method to the study of biology and medicine.</meta>

1

## History of Life and Death.

### Introduction.

**T**hat life is short and art is long <note>original: "De Vita breui, & Arte longâ." This is a famous aphorism originally attributed to the Greek physician Hippocrates (*Ars longa, vita brevis*), reflecting the frustration that human life ends before one can master a craft or science.</note> is an old song and complaint. It seems, therefore, fitting that we, who apply ourselves with all our strength to the perfecting of the **Arts**, should also undertake a consideration of the **prolongation of Human Life**, with the favor of the Author of both Truth and Life <note>A reference to God as the source of both scientific truth and physical existence.</note>. For although the life of mortals,

->A 4<-

<summary>This page introduces Francis Bacon's treatise on longevity, arguing that those dedicated to the sciences should naturally seek ways to extend human life.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, longevity, prolongation of life, Hippocrates, philosophy of science, medical history</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Francis Bacon's "History of Life and Death." Having established that life is short and the arts are long, Bacon begins to reconcile the scientific pursuit of longevity with Christian theology, suggesting that a longer life allows for more good works on earth.</meta>

**Heap and accumulation of Sins and Miseries**;

nevertheless, the **Continuation of Works of Charity** 
is not to be despised, even by us Christians. 
Furthermore, the **Beloved Disciple** <note>original: "Discipulus Amatus"; referring to John the Apostle, who according to tradition lived to a very old age and was the only apostle not to be martyred.</note>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 6, which discussed the traditional view that death occurs because the body’s "Radical Humor" is corrupted. Here, Bacon critiques this view and introduces his own observation: that while the body can find the "matter" to repair itself, the "process" of repair eventually fails.</meta>

[Page number: 7]

[finally] <note>original: "demùm." This word was split across the page break from page 6; "mum" is the concluding syllable.</note> leads the **Depraved** <note>original: "Prauum." Referring to the "degenerate addition" or corrupted humor mentioned on the previous page that eventually leads to the body's total collapse.</note> to 
**Nothing**. They thought these things 
quite unskillfully, and lightly. 
For everything in an Animal, during 
**Adolescence** and **Youth**, 
is repaired entirely; nay, 
even for a time, they are increased in 
**Quantity**, and improved in **Quality**; 
so that the **Matter of Repair** <note>original: "Materia Reparationis." In Bacon’s natural philosophy, this refers to the substance or nutrients available to the body for self-renewal.</note>

if the **Manner of Repair** <note>original: "Modus Reparationis." This refers to the biological mechanism or the "way" in which the body utilizes matter to restore its parts.</note> did not 
fail. But in 
truth, this is what happens. 
As age declines, 
the **Repair** becomes very unequal; 
some parts are repaired 
successfully enough, others 
with difficulty,

<summary>Bacon critiques earlier philosophers' views on aging, arguing that the body possesses enough material for "eternal" repair, but that the process of repair itself becomes uneven and fails as we age.</summary>

with difficulty, and for the worse: so that from that time, human **Bodies** begin to undergo that **torment of Mezentius** <note>original: "tormentum illud Mezentij." In Vergil's *Aeneid*, the tyrant Mezentius executed people by binding them face-to-face with a corpse until they died of infection. Bacon uses this as a metaphor for the living parts of the body being tied to "dead" or hardened parts.</note>, so that the **living** die in the embrace of the **dead**; and those parts **easily repaired**, because they are coupled with those **hardly repaired**, fail as well. when they are dissolved by age, they are dissolved into Rust, not through Perspiration <note>original: *perspirationem*. Bacon uses this to describe the process where internal moisture "breathes out" or evaporates from a substance, leading to decay.</note>; Gold, however, is affected by neither.

avail toward **Durability** <note>Completing the catchword "Dura-" from the previous page.</note>, or **Dissolution**. **I.** | Let it be laid down as a **Postulate** <term>Loco Assumpti; a foundational premise or axiom taken for granted in an argument</term>, which is most certain: that there resides in every **Tangible** body a **Spiri-** <note>This is a catchword, anticipating the word "Spirit" on the following page.</note>

**Spirit** <note>Completing the catchword "Spiri-" from the previous page.</note>, or **pneumatic Body** <term>Corpus pneumaticum; in Bacon’s physics, this refers to a thin, invisible, active substance—distinct from the soul—contained within all tangible matter that governs its internal movements</term>, covered and enclosed by the **tangible parts**; and that from this **Spirit** the beginning of all **Dissolution** and **Consumption** <note>original: *Consumptionis*; the process of a substance being worn away, evaporated, or used up over time</note> is taken; therefore, the **Antidote** to these is the **Detention of the Spirit**.

**2.** | The **Spirit** is detained in two ways: either by **close Compression**, as if in a **Prison**; or by a **Detention** that is, as it were, **spontaneous** <note>original: *spontaneam*; meaning the spirit remains in the body of its own accord rather than by physical force</note>.         C 3        inuita- <note>This is a catchword, anticipating the word "invitatur" (is invited) on the following page.</note>

->Desiccation; the prevention of desiccation; and the softening <note>original: *inteneratio*; a technical term used by Bacon to describe the process of making a dry or hardened substance supple and "tender" again.</note> of that which has been dried.<-

### History.

<margin>To Article 2.</margin>
**F**ire and intense heat dry out some things, but melt others;
<margin>1.</margin>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*As this clay hardens, and as this wax melts,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*In one and the same fire.* <note>original: *Limus ut hic dureſcit, & hæc ut Cera liqueſcit Vno eodemque Igne.* This is a famous line from Virgil’s *Eclogues* (8.80), used here to show that the effect of heat depends entirely on the nature of the material it acts upon.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;Fire dries the earth, and stones, and wood, and cloths, and hides, and all things that do not flow: it melts metals, wax, gums, butter, tallow <term>Seuum</term> <note>Hard animal fat used for making candles and soap.</note>, and the like.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;However <note>This is a "catchword," the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>Bacon begins a systematic "History" of desiccation, observing how the same application of heat can either harden or liquefy a substance depending on its composition. He quotes Virgil to illustrate that scientific laws must account for the specific properties of the matter being acted upon.</summary>
<keywords>desiccation, heat, thermodynamics, Virgil, material properties, liquefaction, hardening, Francis Bacon, tallow</keywords>

   | (except *Gold* <note>Bacon correctly observes that gold is a noble metal that does not oxidize or lose weight when heated in standard metallurgical processes.</note>) and become

   | and *fatty* substances, by a stronger *Fire*, become
   | fried, and toasted, and more dry,
   | and crusted.
__________________________________________________
3. | *Air*, especially open air, manifestly
   | dries and never liquefies; just as when the
   | roads, and the Surface of the Earth,
   | moistened by Rains, are dried out;
   | washed Linens, which are exposed
   | to the *Air*, are dried; Herbs, and Leaves, and
   | Flowers, are dried in the shade. But
   | *Air* does this much more so if it is
   | brightened by the rays of the Sun, (provided
   | it does not lead <note>original: *inducat*; this is a "catchword," a printer's tool that repeats the first word of the next page to ensure the sheets are bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that even materials that melt (like metals and fats) will eventually dry out and become brittle if the heat is intense enough to drive off their "volatile" parts. He also identifies open air as a universal drying agent, noting its effect on everything from roads to laundry.</summary>
<keywords>desiccation, fire, metallurgy, gold, volatile spirits, evaporation, air, solar heat, preservation</keywords>

->History of Life and Death.                                 43<-

<note>Continued from previous page: "...provided it does not lead to"</note> lead to Putrefaction <note>In Bacon’s philosophy, putrefaction is the "corruption" of a body by trapped moisture and heat, whereas desiccation is a clean drying out.</note>) or if the air is in motion; as when the Winds blow, and in breezy Open Spaces.

<margin>4.</margin> **Age** <note>original: *Ætas*; referring to the passage of time or old age.</note> dries things out most of all, yet most slowly; as happens in all Bodies which, through Old Age (provided they are not overtaken by Putrefaction), become parched. However, **Age** is nothing in itself (since it is only a Measure of Time), but the Effect is produced by the innate **Spirit** <term>Spiritus</term> <note>Bacon uses "spirit" to describe a subtle, invisible, and active substance—like a gas—that he believed resided within all tangible bodies, governing their preservation or decay.</note> of the Bodies. This spirit sucks out the Moisture of the Body and flies away <note>original: *evolat*; evaporates or escapes.</note> together with it. The effect is also caused by the surrounding **Air**, which multiplies itself <note>Bacon suggests the outer air "preys upon" or draws out the inner spirits and juices, a process of chemical or physical attraction.</note> upon the innate spirits and the Juices of the Body, and preys upon them.

<margin>5.</margin> **Cold**, of all things, dries most properly; since Desiccation <note>The process of drying out completely.</note> does not occur except through Contraction;
                      D <note>This "D" is a signature mark used by printers to keep the gathered pages in the correct order for binding.</note>             which <note>original: *quod*; this is a catchword, repeating the first word of the following page.</note>
__________________________________________________________

<summary>Bacon explains that while time itself is just a measurement, "Age" dries objects through the slow escape of internal spirits and the predatory action of the surrounding air. He also identifies cold as a primary cause of drying through the physical contraction of matter.</summary>
<keywords>desiccation, putrefaction, Age, Spirits, Air, Cold, contraction, Francis Bacon, natural philosophy</keywords>

In early modern science, saltpeter (potassium nitrate) was believed to contain a "frigid spirit" and was used in early attempts at refrigeration.</note>; therefore **Desiccations** <note>the process of drying out</note> **from Cold** are feeble, and easily undone: yet we see the **Face of the Earth** dried by **Frost**, and by **March Winds** more than by the **Sun**; since the same **Wind**, which laps up the **Moisture**, also strikes in the **Cold**.

<meta>This page transitions from the prevention of dryness (desiccation) to the "softening" or "re-moistening" (inteneration) of materials that have already become brittle. This is a crucial shift in Bacon's argument, moving from mere preservation to the active restoration of youthfulness in physical bodies.</meta>

*History of Life and Death.* [Page number] 55
__________________________________________________________________
The **Softening of the Dried** <note>original: "Inteneratio Desiccati"; the process of restoring moisture and suppleness to biological or physical materials that have become parched or brittle with age, which Bacon considers the primary goal of his study on longevity.</note> (which is the most important matter) provides few **Experiments**; therefore, we will join together some observations which occur in **Animals**, and also in **Man**.

<margin>27</margin> **Willow twigs**, which are used for binding **Trees**, become more flexible when soaked in **Water**; similarly, the ends of **Fennel stalks** <note>original: "Ferulae"; the stalks of the giant fennel plant, often used as canes or rods.</note> are placed in **Pitchers** with **Water** so that they do not dry out; furthermore, **Gaming-balls** <note>original: "Globuli lusorij"; likely wooden bowls or balls used in lawn games that were prone to splitting.</note>, even if they have developed cracks through **Dryness**, are filled out again and made solid when placed in **Water**.

<margin>28</margin> **Boots** made of **Leather** that have become hard and stubborn with age are softened by the rubbing-in of **Tallow** <note>original: "Seui"; rendered animal fat used as a lubricant and waterproofing agent.</note> near a **Fire**; they also soften somewhat even when just brought near a simple **Fire**. **Bladders** and **Membranes**, after... <note>The text ends here at the catchword "quam," indicating the start of the next page.</note>

<summary>Bacon begins discussing the "softening" (inteneration) of dry materials, a key process for reversing the effects of age. He provides everyday examples of how water and heat can restore flexibility to willow, wood, and leather.</summary>
<keywords>Inteneration, restoration, willow twigs, fennel stalks, leather boots, tallow, moisture, flexibility</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of observations regarding the "softening" or restoration of dried-out materials (inteneration), providing examples from botany, animal husbandry, and medicine to support Bacon's theory that physical rejuvenation is possible through the application of moisture and nutrients.</meta>

56 | *History of Life and Death.*
--- | ---
&nbsp; | no matter how much they have been hardened, are softened <note>original: "intenerantur"; a key term in Bacon’s biology referring to the restoration of suppleness to "dried" or aged tissue.</note> by **warm Water** mixed with **Tallow** <note>original: "Seuo"; rendered animal fat, typically from sheep or cattle, used for its penetrating and moisturizing properties.</note>, or some other **Fatty substance**; but they are softened better still if they are also rubbed a little.
29 | Very old **Trees**, which have stood motionless for a long time, by digging and opening the earth around their **Roots**, clearly grow young again, as it were, putting forth **new and tender Leaves**.
30 | Old **Plow-Oxen**, completely exhausted by their labors, when led into **lush Pastures**, are clothed with **Flesh** that is new, tender, and youthful, so that in **Taste** they even resemble the **meat of young Bullocks**.
31 | A strict, consuming, and emaciating **Diet** consisting of **Guaiacum** <note>original: "Guaiaco"; a medicinal wood from the Caribbean used in the 17th century for "sweat-treatments" to purge the body of diseases like syphilis.</note>, **twice-baked Bread** <note>original: "Pane bis cocto"; similar to hardtack or rusk, used in restrictive medical diets to reduce "humors."</note>, and similar things (such as is used for curing...

<summary>Bacon provides empirical examples of rejuvenation, noting how old trees flourish when their roots are disturbed and how exhausted oxen regain youthful flesh in good pastures. He also introduces the medical use of restrictive diets as a precursor to physical renewal.</summary>
<keywords>inteneration, restoration, roots, rejuvenation, plow-oxen, Guaiacum, medical diet, physical renewal</keywords>

### *Major Observations.*

**In strange ways Men**, <margin>1.</margin> like **Owls** <note>Bacon frequently uses the owl as a metaphor for philosophers who are comfortable in the "darkness" of abstract theory but are blinded by the "light" of actual nature and experimental evidence.</note> in the **Darkness of their own Notions**, [behave]

<summary>Bacon argues that extreme medical diets and emaciating diseases can paradoxically prolong life by clearing out old bodily fluids and allowing for the growth of "fresh" youthful tissue. Concerning **Thinning**, the matter is clear; for the **spirit** <term>spirit: In Bacon's physics, 'spirit' is a pressurized, air-like substance found inside all matter that seeks to expand or escape.</term>, which is enclosed in every **tangible body** <term>tangible body: Solid or liquid matter that can be felt, distinguished from the invisible and weightless 'spirits' that reside within it.</term>, does not forget itself <note>original: "sui non obliviscitur"; a personification suggesting that the spirit naturally acts according to its own drive to expand.</note>; but whatever it finds in the body (in which it is trapped) that it can digest, work upon, and transform into itself, it completely alters and subdues; and from that material, it multiplies itself and generates new spirit. <summary>Bacon explains that the internal spirit of a body naturally seeks to expand by consuming and converting the surrounding solid matter into more spirit, which results in the body losing weight and becoming porous.</summary>

Porous, and resonant from within <note>original: "ab intùs Sonantia." ...not enough of the **Body** <note>original: "Corpus." In Bacon’s natural philosophy, the "body" is the tangible material substance of an object, which works in tension with the invisible "spirits" within it.</note> remains to unite and **Contract** itself, then indeed **Contraction** necessarily ceases, and the **Body** is rendered rotten, and becomes nothing more than **Coherent Dust** <note>original: "Pulvisculus cohærens." This occurs in all bodies that are extremely wasted away; in **Paper** and **Linen** that have been burnt to the very end; and in **Embalmed Corpses** after many centuries have passed. | 7. | It should be noted that **Fire** and **Heat** dry things only by **Accident** <note>original: "per Accidens." In the natural philosophy of this era, an "accidental" effect is a secondary result or side-effect, whereas a "proper" effect is the primary, direct action of a force.</note>; for their proper Work is to **thin out** and **expand** the **Spirit** and the **Moistures**; it follows, however, as an accidental result, that the remaining parts **contract** themselves; whether this is solely to **Shun a Vacuum** <note>original: "Fugam Vacui." This refers to the traditional physical principle that "nature abhors a vacuum," meaning matter will naturally pull together or rush in to ensure no empty space is left behind.</note>, or due to some other **Motion** occurring at the same time; of which we are not speaking now. |
| 8. | It is also certain that **Putrefaction** <note>original: "Putrefactionem." To Bacon, decay or rotting is a process where the internal "spirits" of a body become disorganized and escape, leading to the collapse of the material's structural integrity.</note>, no less than |

[Catchword] the <note>The catchword "the" (translated from "quam") indicates that the next page will continue the comparison between putrefaction and other forms of drying or decay.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that heat does not dry matter directly, but rather expands the internal "spirits" until they leave, forcing the remaining solid matter to shrink. He begins to compare this mechanical process to the biological process of decay.</summary>

# Longevity and the 
# Shortness of Life 
# in Animals.

# *History.* <note>original: "Historia." For Bacon, a "History" is not a narrative of the past, but a "Natural History"—a systematic collection of data and observations used to build a scientific understanding of a subject.</note>

<margin>In reference to Article 3. Connection.</margin>

**C**oncerning the Long-lastingness and Shortness of Life in Animals, the information that can be obtained is thin; observation is careless; tradition is full of fables <note>original: "Traditio fabulosa." Bacon often complains that historical accounts of animal ages (like the long-lived stag) are based on myths rather than verified data.</note>. In domesticated animals <note>original: "Cicuribus." The **Donkey** is of nearly the same **age** as the Horse; but the **Mule** is more **long-lived** than either of them.

**8.** | The life of **Deer** is commonly celebrated for its **length**; yet there is no **certain account** of it. They tell some story of a **Collared Deer**, <note>original: "Ceruo Torquato." This refers to a popular historical legend where a deer was reportedly found with a gold collar placed on it by a figure like Alexander the Great or Julius Caesar, implying the animal had lived for hundreds of years.</note> where the **collar** itself was covered by the **fatness of its flesh**. This **Longevity** in the **Deer** is rendered less credible because the animal reaches **full maturity** by its **Fifth Year**; and not long after, the **horns** (which fall off and are renewed every year) begin to grow back **closer together** at the forehead and are **less branched**. <note>Bacon often argues that the time it takes for an animal to reach its full physical growth is a reliable indicator of its total lifespan; since deer mature quickly, he suspects their reputed long life is a myth.</note>

**23** The **Eagle** is held to be **long-lived**;

**Eagle**: For after the beak,
by its own curvature, has grown too much,

<meta>This page follows the survey of aquatic life on page 90, transitioning from specific animal examples to "Greater Observations"—Bacon’s synthesis of broader patterns and rules governing the lifespan of various species.</meta>

_History of Life and Death._ 91

### Greater Observations.

It is difficult to find any definite rule <note>original: "Normam," meaning a standard, pattern, or yardstick.</note> for the length or brevity of life in animals, owing to both a lack of careful observation and the complexity of causes involved. We shall note a few points.

<margin>1</margin> More long-lived species are found among **birds** than among **quadrupeds** <note>original: "Quadrupedibus," meaning four-legged land mammals.</note>; such as the **Eagle**, **Vulture**, **Kite**, **Pelican**, **Raven**, **Crow**, **Swan**, **Goose**, **Stork**, **Crane**, **Ibis**, **Parrot**, 
G <note>This letter serves as a catchword, indicating the beginning of the next page’s text.</note>

<summary>Bacon begins his general synthesis of animal longevity, noting that while biological rules are hard to define due to poor data, birds generally outlive four-legged mammals.</summary>
<keywords>longevity, animal lifespan, birds, quadrupeds, Francis Bacon, natural history, observation</keywords>

Certainly, the covering <note>original: "Tegumentum," referring to their feathers.</note> of birds is an excellent defense against the harshness of the weather; and since they live for the most part in the open air, they are similar to the inhabitants of the purer mountains, who are known to be long-lived. 102 [tab] *History of Life and Death.*

**Quantity** itself contributes much to the **Preservation of the Whole**: <note>original: "Conseruationem Integri." Bacon is positing that a larger mass of matter is more resilient against the forces of decay and environmental change than a smaller one.</note> For a **great Fire** takes a longer time to be extinguished; a **small portion of Water** evaporates more quickly; a tree **Trunk** does not dry out as fast as a **Twig**; Therefore, generally (I speak of **Species**, not of **Individuals**), those **Animals** that are larger in **Bulk** are longer-lived than **Small ones**; unless some other powerful **Cause** should hinder the matter.

*History of Life and Death.*

# **NOURISHMENT**
## **and the**
## **Way of Nourishing.**

### **The History.**

<margin>Related to Article 4, Observation 1.</margin>
**Nourishment**, in relation to the **Thing Nourished**, ought to be of an **inferior Nature** and a **simpler substance**: <note>original: "Alimentum erga Alimentatum debet esse Naturæ inferioris." Bacon is describing a hierarchy of consumption where the consumer must be more complex than the consumed for assimilation to occur.</note> **Plants** are nourished by **Earth** and **Water**; **Animals** from **Plants**; **Men** from **Animals**. There are also **carnivorous Animals**, and **Man** himself takes **Plants** as part of his **Nourishment**; but **Man**, and **carnivorous Animals**, are nourished with difficulty from **Plants** alone; they can perhaps [sustain themselves] from **Fruits**, and **Seeds** cooked by **Fire**, <note>Bacon suggests that while humans can eat plants, raw vegetation is insufficient for complex animals without the "ripening" effect of fire or the pre-processing provided by the bodies of other animals.</note>

<summary>Bacon outlines the hierarchical chain of nutrition, asserting that organisms must consume substances simpler than themselves—such as plants consuming earth, and humans consuming animals—to maintain life.</summary>
<keywords>nourishment, nutrition, hierarchy of nature, diet, plants, carnivores, digestion, assimilation, Francis Bacon, natural history</keywords>

Indeed, if they are stripped of their bark in a circle <note>A process known as "girdling," which disrupts the flow of nutrients.</note>, even for a [Margin: 8] small space, they live no longer. Furthermore, the blood in the veins of animals nourishes the flesh situated beneath them no less than that which is above them.

In all nourishment there is a twofold action: *extrusion* <note>The process of "pushing out" nutrients to the body's parts.</note> and *attraction* <note>The process of the parts "drawing in" those nutrients.</note>; [Margin: 6] of which the first proceeds from an internal function [Margin: e] and the second from an external one.

Vegetables <note>In the 17th century, this term referred to all plant life.</note> assimilate their nourishment [Margin: 7] simply, without excretion: for gums and "tears" <term>Tears: In botanical terms, the natural exudation of sap or resin from a plant's bark.</term> are rather signs of abundance <note>original: "exuberantiæ"</note> than of waste <note>Bacon is arguing that plants are so efficient at processing nutrients that they do not produce "excrement" in the same way animals do; their sap is merely an overflow of good material.</note>.

<margin>10</margin> In animals, there are stages of nourishment according to age; for the fetus in the womb, the maternal fluid <note>original: "Succus Maternus"; referring to the nutrients supplied via the placenta and amniotic fluid.</note> is sufficient; from birth, milk; thereafter, food and drink; and in old age, coarser and more savory foods are generally pleasing.

<margin>11</margin> It is of the greatest importance to the present inquiry to investigate diligently and attentively whether nourishment cannot occur from the outside, or at least by some way other than the mouth. ### History.

<margin>Regarding Articles 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, and 11. 1</margin>
Holy Scripture reports that before the Flood, men lived for many centuries of years; yet none of the Patriarchs <note>original: "Patrum"; the "Fathers" or biblical figures mentioned in the Book of Genesis.</note> completed their thousandth year. Nor was this length of life... <note>The word "Gra-" is a catchword leading to the next page, likely beginning a discussion on the gradual decline of human lifespan.</note>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the "History" section regarding the lifespan of the biblical Patriarchs, specifically comparing the lineage of Seth (the "Holy Lineage") with that of Cain, and tracking the decline of longevity following the Great Flood.</meta>

History of Life and Death. [Page number in box:] 111

Grace, or the Holy Lineage <note>original: "Gratiæ, aut Lineæ Sanctæ"; referring to the theological idea that long life was a divine gift to the righteous line of Seth.</note>, could be the cause [of such longevity]; since there are recorded, before the Flood, eleven generations of the Patriarchs; but of the sons of Adam through Cain, there are only eight generations; so that the progeny of Cain might even seem to be the longer-lived <note>Bacon notes an interesting chronological paradox: because Cain's line had fewer generations over the same period, each individual must have lived longer on average than those in the "holy" line.</note>. However, this longevity, immediately after the Flood, collapsed by half; but only in those born after the event. For Noah, who had been born before the Flood, matched the age of his ancestors; and Shem reached his six-hundredth year. Then, after three generations from the Flood, the life of men was reduced to nearly a fourth part of the primitive age; namely, to about two hundred years.

<margin>2</margin> **Abraham** lived one hundred and seventy-five years; a man of great spirit <note>original: "Magnanimus"; meaning great-souled or courageous.</note>, and one to whom all things yielded <note>The catchword "debant" completes the word "cedebant" from the following page.</note>

H 3

# History of Life and Death. [127]

...trusting [in Fortune], and in his **Youth** having experienced many **vicissitudes of fortune**, yet throughout the **course of his Age**, he was **consistently fortunate**.

But **Marcus Porcius Cato** <note>Known as Cato the Elder (234–149 BCE), a Roman statesman famous for his strict morality and "Carthage must be destroyed" refrain</note> lived beyond his **ninetieth year**; a man of an almost **iron Body** and **Mind**; of a **bitter Tongue**, and a lover of **rivalries**; he was likewise devoted to **Agriculture**, and acted as the **Physician** to **himself** and to his **Family** <note>Cato famously distrusted Greek doctors and preferred traditional Roman herbal remedies and cabbage to treat his household</note>.

<margin>13</margin> **Terentia, the wife of Cicero**, lived to her **hundred and third year**; a woman afflicted by **many hardships**: first by the **Exile** of her husband, then by their **Divorce**, and again by his **ultimate calamity** <note>Referring to Cicero's execution and the display of his head and hands in the Roman Forum</note>; she was also frequently troubled by **gout** <note>A painful form of arthritis historically associated with rich diets, though Bacon notes it here as a condition survived into extreme old age</note>. **Luceia** surpassed her **hundredth year** by no small margin; since it is said that for a full **hundred years**, she performed as an **Actress** on the **Stage**

# History of Life and Death. | 129

**Augusta**, the wife of **Caesar Augustus** and mother of **Tiberius**. For indeed, if the **Life of Augustus** was a **Play** (which he himself intended, since while lying on his deathbed he had instructed his **Friends** that, after he had breathed his last, they should offer him the **"Plaudite"** <note>original: "Plaudite," the traditional request for applause at the end of a Roman theatrical performance. Augustus's reported last words were: "Since I have played my part well, all clap your hands and dismiss me with applause."</note>), then certainly **Livia** was the greatest **Actress**. She adapted herself so well, showing **Compliance** to her **Husband**, yet a certain **Power** and **Predominance** toward her **Son**. She was a woman of **Courtesy**, yet of **Matronly** gravity; **Active** in affairs, and **tenacious of Power**.

But **Junia**, the wife of **Caius Cassius** and sister of **Marcus Brutus**, was also a **Nonagenarian** <note>A person in their nineties</note>; she lived for **sixty-four years** after the **Battle of Philippi** <note>The decisive battle in 42 BCE where the forces of Brutus and Cassius were defeated by Octavian and Mark Antony</note>. She was a woman of **Great Spirit**, happy in her **Wealth**, though mournful due to the **Calamity** of her **Husband** and **Kinsmen**, and her **long Widowhood**; yet for all that, she was held in **Honor**.

15 . | The seventy-sixth **year of our Lord** <note>original: "Annus Domini Septuagesimus Sextus"</note> is memorable, in the time of the **Emperor Vespasian**, in which records of **Longevity** are found, serving as if they were **Public Registers** <note>original: "Fasti," referring to the official Roman calendars or records of events</note>. **Polycarp**, a disciple of the Apostles and **Bishop of Smyrna**, seems to have extended his life to a hundred years and more, even though he was eventually cut short by **martyrdom**; he was a man of lofty spirit, heroic patience, and untiring in his labors. **Dionysius the Areopagite**, a contemporary of the **Apostle Paul**, appears to have lived to ninety years.    The accounts that follow will be presented in mixed order, are of more doubtful reliability, and are based on more meager observation. 19

148          *History of Life and Death.*

(Conscript Fathers,) I have borne my blindness for many years now; but now I could wish that I were deaf as well, since I hear you entertaining such shameful proposals <note>original: *Cæcitatem meam... Surdum quoque optauerim*; Appius’s famous rebuke to the Senate for considering a peace treaty with the invader Pyrrhus, suggesting that hearing the disgrace was worse than being unable to see.</note>.

*Marcus Perpenna* <note>Marcus Perpenna, a Roman statesman who served as consul in 92 BCE</note> lived ninety-eight years. He survived everyone whose opinion he had requested in the Senate during his year as consul <term>Consul<gloss>One of the two highest-ranking magistrates in the Roman Republic</gloss></term> (that is, all the Senators of his year). He even outlived all those whom he had shortly thereafter enrolled in the Senate while serving as censor <term>Censor<gloss>An official responsible for maintaining the census and overseeing public morality and the composition of the Senate</gloss></term>, with only seven individuals excepted.

*Hiero*, King of Sicily <note>Hiero II, the long-reigning King of Syracuse and a vital ally to Rome</note>, lived to almost his hundredth year during the times of the Second Punic War. He was a man moderate in both his governance and his personal conduct; a devoted worshiper of the divine powers, a faithful preserver of friendship, generous, and consistently favored by fortune.

He was a man learned with a certain practical wisdom; extensive in experience and observation; not grasping for mere words or empty methods, but isolating and setting forth only the essential sinews of knowledge <note>original: *neruos tantùm Scientiæ*; Bacon uses the metaphor of "sinews" to describe the vital, functional parts of medical knowledge as opposed to useless theoretical "fat."</note>.

*Demonax* the *Philosopher* (not only by profession, but by his very character), lived in the time of Hadrian <note>Publius Aelius Hadrianus, Roman Emperor from 117–138 CE. They replied, "Is it your wish, then, to be exposed to birds or dogs?" He answered, "Since I have striven with all my might to be of benefit to men while alive, what harm is there <note>original: *quæ inuidia eſt*; literally "what envy/ill-will is there," suggesting he does not begrudge his body to the animals.</note> if, when dead, I also provide something to animals?"

The people of India called the *Pandorae* are remarkably long-lived; some even reaching their two hundredth year. <note>The Pandorae were a legendary tribe mentioned by ancient geographers like Pliny the Elder; they were said to dwell in the mountains and possess unusual physical traits.</note>

20  Among us in **England**, I believe there does not exist a village, even if only slightly populated, in which there cannot be found some man or woman among the octogenarians <note>original: *Octogenariis* — people in their eighties.</note>. Even a few years ago in the county of **Herefordshire**, during the **May-games** <note>original: *Ludos Florales* — literally "Floral Games," referring to traditional English spring festivals and Morris dancing.</note>, a dance was arranged consisting of eight men whose ages, when added together, reached eight hundred years; for what some of them lacked of reaching a century, others exceeded.

21  In **Bethlehem Hospital** <note>Better known as "Bedlam," this was the primary institution for the mentally ill in London.</note>, in the suburbs of **London**, which was established for the support and custody of the mentally ill <note>original: *Phreneticorum* — those suffering from "phrensy" or acute mental disturbance.</note>, many of those deprived of their senses <note>original: *Mente captis* — a legal and medical term for the "insane."</note> are found from time to time to have been long-lived.

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s observations on how geography and climate influence human longevity, shifting from the historical stability of the human race to the environmental factors that preserve the "vital spirits."</meta>

24 | In colder and *Northern* <note>original: *Hyperboreis* — A term from Greek mythology referring to people living "beyond the North Wind," used here to describe Arctic or sub-Arctic climates.</note> regions, men generally live longer than in *hotter* ones. This must necessarily happen because the skin is more tightened; the *humors* <note>original: *Succi Corporis* — literally "juices of the body," the vital fluids that early modern science believed maintained health and suppleness.</note> of the body are less likely to evaporate; and the *Spirits* <note>original: *Spiritus* — In Bacon’s physiology, "vital spirits" were a vaporous substance that animated the body; he believed they could either "consume" the body’s moisture through heat or "repair" it through steady movement.</note> themselves are less sharp in consuming the body, and more industrious in repairing it. Furthermore, the air (being only moderately warmed by the rays of the Sun) is less *predatory* <note>original: *Prædatorius* — Bacon frequently described the atmosphere as a "predator" that leached away the body's natural moisture.</note>. 25 | *Islanders* generally live longer than those on the *mainland* <note>original: *Mediterraneis* — In this context, it refers to those living in the "middle of the lands" (inland/continental) rather than the inhabitants of the Mediterranean coast.</note> for the most part...

<summary>Bacon explains that cold climates promote longevity by tightening the skin and slowing the consumption of the body’s "spirits," while also noting that stable equatorial environments can be equally beneficial.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the physical descriptions from page 173, completing the word "minutius" (smaller) which refers to the size of the head in proportion to the body as a sign of long life.</meta>

smaller <note>original: *minutius* — this completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the head's size in proportion to the body.</note>; a **Neck** of moderate size, not long, or slender, or swollen, or as if tucked into the **Shoulders**: **Nostrils** that are wide, regardless of the shape of the **Nose**: a **Mouth** somewhat large: an ***Ear*** that is cartilaginous, not **Fleshy**; ***Teeth*** that are strong and close together, not thin or widely spaced, foretell **Longevity**; and much more so if some ***new Teeth*** should emerge in **advanced Age**.

*History of Life and Death.* [box] 177

The **Fuller** diet <note>original: *Plenior*</note> provides copious nourishment, from which it repairs the body more;
the **Middle** diet provides neither. For where **Extremes** are harmful, the **Middle** is best; but where the **Extremes** are helpful, the **Middle** is almost of no value.
Moreover, for that **Stricter Diet**, **Wakefulness** <note>original: *Vigilia*</note> is also suitable, lest the few **Spirits** <note>In Bacon’s physiology, "spirits" were subtle, vaporous substances that governed bodily functions; too much sleep could "drown" or stifle them if they were already low due to fasting.</note> be overwhelmed by too much **Sleep**;
likewise **Moderate Exercise** is needed, lest they be dissipated; and **Abstinence from Sex** <note>original: *Veneris Abstinentia* — literally "abstinence from Venus."</note>, lest they be exhausted.
On the contrary, for the **Abundant Diet**, a more generous **Sleep**, more frequent **Exercise**, and the **Timely Use of Sex** are suitable.
**Baths** and **Ointments** (such as have been in use) were suited more for **Luxury** <note>original: *Deliciis*</note> rather than for **Prolonging Life**.
But concerning all these matters, when we come to the **Inquiry**

When we come to the **Intentions** <note>In this work, Bacon uses "Intentions" to mean the specific physiological goals or methods he proposes for extending life, such as "the cooling of the spirits" or "the repair of the parts."</note>, we shall speak more accurately. In the meantime, the advice of **Celsus** <note>Aulus Cornelius Celsus (c. 25 BC – c. 50 AD), a Roman author whose medical encyclopedia was highly influential during the Renaissance for its practical wisdom.</note> the Physician—who was not only learned but also wise—is not to be despised. He commands **Variety** and **Alternation** in diet, but with an inclination toward the more generous side: Namely, that one should sometimes accustom oneself to **Waking Hours**, and at other times indulge in **Sleep**, but in sleep more often; likewise, sometimes one should **Fast**, and sometimes **Feast**, but feast more often; sometimes one should apply oneself vigorously to **Mental Exertions** <note>original: *Animi Contentionibus* — refers to intense study or concentrated thought.</note>, and sometimes use **Relaxations**, but relaxations more often. It is certainly beyond doubt that a well-ordered **Diet** holds the primary role in prolonging life; nor is it conven-

<margin>7</margin> We also judge that certain wasting diseases <note>original: "Morbos Emaciantes." By *Flight*, by far the most powerful and effective [agent] is *Opium*; and then *Opiates*, and generally *Soporifics* <note>term: "Soporifics" (soporifera). Substances that induce or produce sleep.</note>.

<margin>15</margin> The effectiveness of *Opium* for the *Condensation of the Spirits* is quite remarkable; since perhaps three *Grains* <note>term: "Grains" (grana). A traditional unit of weight based on a single seed of cereal; in 17th-century medicine, it was roughly 65 milligrams.</note> of it can, shortly after ingestion, so coagulate the *Spirits* that they do not return, but are extinguished and rendered immobile.

<margin>16</margin> *Opium* and similar substances do not put the *Spirits* to flight because of their own *Coldness* (for they clearly possess warm parts); but, on the contrary, they cool the body because of the *Flight of the Spirits* <note>note: Bacon is challenging the ancient "Galenic" view that opium worked because it was inherently "cold." He argues instead that it causes a mechanical reaction where the spirits flee, and the loss of spirits results in the sensation of cold.</note>.

<margin>17</margin> The *Flight of the Spirits* caused by *Opium* and *Opiates* is best observed when they are applied *externally*; because the *Spirits* immediately withdraw themselves and no longer wish to approach, but [catchword:] mortify— <note>note: The catchword "morti-" suggests the text on the following page will describe how the flesh becomes "mortified" or numbed when spirits flee from a localized area treated with opium.</note>

<summary>Bacon identifies opium as the most potent substance for condensing the vital spirits through "flight." He argues that its cooling effect is a secondary result of the spirits withdrawing from the substance rather than an inherent property of the drug itself.</summary>

These are complex pharmacological recipes involving many ingredients, with opium acting as the primary active component.</note> are: **Theriac**, **Mithridate**, **Triferas**, **Paracelsus's Laudanum**, **Diacodium**, **Diascordium**, **Philonium**, and **Pills of Hound's-tongue** <note>original: "Pillulæ de Cyno-gloßâ". These were historical medicines used for coughs and as sleep aids; Theriac and Mithridate were "universal antidotes" containing dozens of ingredients.</note>.

<margin>31</margin> From these things which have been said, certain **Designations** or **Counsels** for the **prolongation of life** could be derived, according to this **Intention**: namely, the **Condensation of the Spirits** through **Opiates** <note>Bacon's central theory is that life is a "flame" that consumes the body; by using opiates to "condense" or thicken the spirits, one can slow down this consumption and live longer.</note>.

<margin>32</margin> Therefore, let there be every year from **Mature Youth** onward, a certain **Opiate Diet**. Let it be taken near the **End of May**; because in **Summer** the **Spirits** are most dissolved <note>original: "soluuntur". In early modern physiology, heat was thought to thin and scatter the vital spirits, leading to exhaustion and aging.</note> and thinned, and there is less fear of **cold Humors** <note>Taking cooling opiates in winter was considered dangerous because the body was already cold; May was the ideal time to begin a "cooling" regimen to prepare for the summer heat.</note>:

<summary>Bacon lists various natural and compound narcotics, including hemlock and laudanum, before proposing a seasonal medical regimen. He suggests an "opiate diet" starting in late May to condense the vital spirits and protect them from being thinned by the summer heat.</summary>

**History of Life and Death.** [space] **227**

[Marginal note: 04] <note>The marginal numbering in the original source may be inconsistent; this section continues the investigation into the condensation of spirits.</note> we shall investigate; for the proper work of **Cold** is **Condensation** <term>Condensation: The process of making the vital spirits thicker and more durable, preventing them from "burning up" the body's resources too quickly.</term>; and it is accomplished without any **Malignity** <note>original: "Malignitate." Meaning harmful or poisonous side effects.</note> or **Hostile Quality**: and therefore the operation is safer than through **Opiates**; although it is a little less powerful if it were used only occasionally, just as **Opiates** are used. [Marginal note: 39] The **Cooling of the Spirits** <term>Vital Spirits: In Bacon's philosophy, these are the active, vaporous substances within the body that drive all biological processes; keeping them cool prevents premature aging.</term> is done in three ways: either by **Respiration**; or by **Vapors**; or by **Nourishment**. The **First** <note>Respiration.</note> is best, but almost beyond our power <note>Likely referring to the difficulty of controlling the temperature or quality of the ambient air one breathes constantly.</note>; the **Second** <note>Vapors.</note> is also powerful, and yet it is at hand; the **Third** <note>Nourishment.</note> is weak and works through **Circuits** <note>original: "per Circuitus." Meaning a roundabout way, where the cooling effect must pass through the digestive system before reaching the spirits.</note>.

*Air* <note>original catchword: "Aer." This indicates the next page will likely begin a discussion on the quality of the air.</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that using cold to condense the vital spirits is safer and more effective for longevity than using opiates, as it can be integrated into a daily diet. He identifies three methods for cooling these spirits: breathing, inhaling vapors, and consuming specific foods.</summary>

History of Life and Death. 235

[The text continues from the previous page:] ...they are well taken infused in a drink, <margin>87</margin> or raw.

Just as the condensation <margin>57</margin> of the **Spirit** <note>Bacon refers to the "vital spirit," a subtle, airy substance he believed resided in the body and drove all biological processes; preserving it was the key to longevity.</note> through substances subordinate to **Opium** <note>Bacon categorized medicines by their effects; "subordinate to Opium" refers to mild sedatives that mimic opium's ability to thicken or "condense" the spirits, but without its toxicity.</note> is achieved to some extent through scents; so <margin>92</margin> too is that condensation which is achieved through substances subordinate to **Nitre** <note>Bacon viewed Nitre (saltpeter) as the ultimate "cooler" for the spirits. Subordinate substances are milder botanical alternatives that share this cooling nature.</note>. Therefore, the scent of fresh <margin>63</margin> and pure earth best restrains the Spirits, whether by following a plow, or by digging, or by pulling up weeds; also the leaves in woods and hedges, <margin>62</margin> as Autumn declines, when falling, provide a good **Refreshment** <note>original: "Refrigerium." In this context, it refers to a cooling process that prevents the vital spirits from becoming too hot and "preying" upon the body's tissues.</note> to the Spirits; and most of all, the dying Strawberry plant. Likely from the Italian *madreselva*, referring to woodbine or honeysuckle.</note>, caught while they are growing, is similar in nature.

58 | Furthermore, we have known a certain noble man of great age <note>Bacon frequently cites anecdotal evidence of longevity to support his theories on "vital spirits."</note> who, immediately upon waking, ordered a clod of **Fresh Earth** to be placed beneath his nostrils daily, so that he might inhale its scent.

59 | There is no doubt that the **Cooling** and tempering of the blood through cold substances—such as **Endive**, **Chicory**, **Liverwort**, **Purslane**, and the like—consequently cools the spirits as well. Bacon argues that eating cooling herbs affects the spirits only after being processed by the liver and blood, whereas breathing scents (vapors) affects the spirits instantly.</note>; whereas vapors act immediately.

We stated that the third method of condensation is through what we call **Soothing** <note>original: "Demulcere." This implies a gentle, smoothing action to reduce the "harshness" or agitation of the vital spirits.</note> the Spirits. The fourth method is through the **Calming** of their alacrity and excessive motion.

63 | The **Heat of the Spirits**, as we have said, must be of such a kind that it is **Robust**, not **Sharp** <note>original: "Robustus, non Acris." In Bacon's theory, "robust" heat is a steady, strengthening force, while "sharp" heat is volatile and destructive.</note>; and it should prefer to break down **stubborn** matter <note>original: "Obstinata subruere." This refers to the spirits' role in digesting and overcoming the more solid, resistant parts of the body to facilitate renewal.</note>, rather than carrying away those parts that are already thinned out.

64 | One must be cautious of **Spices**, **Wine**, and **Strong Drink**; their use should be extremely temperate and interrupted by periods of abstinence. One should also avoid **Savory**, **Oregano**, **Pennyroyal**, and all things which are **Sharp** and **Inflaming** to the palate. For these things provide the Spirits with a Heat that is not **Workmanlike** <note>original: "Fabrilem." Bacon uses this term to describe a heat that acts like a craftsman or blacksmith, constructively refining the body's substances.</note>, but **Predatory** <note>original: "Prædatorium." This is heat that "preys" upon the body's vital moisture, drying it out and hastening decay.</note>.

65 | A **Robust Heat** is provided especially by **Elecampane**, **Garlic**, **Blessed Thistle**, **young Nasturtium**, **Germander**, **Angelica**, **Zedoary**, **Vervain**, **Valerian**, **Myrrh**, **Costus**, **Elderflowers**, and **Sweet Cicely** <note>original: "Myrrhis." A fragrant herb often used in traditional medicine for its warming properties.</note>.

Of these

<margin>72</margin> It must also be observed that a body which is more fully nourished, and is not emaciated by the types of diets we have described, should not omit the *timely use of Venus* <note>original: "Veneris vsum tempestiuum." **86** | **Envy** is the worst of the affections, for it preys upon the **Spirits**, and they in turn prey upon the **Body**; it is all the more dangerous because it is almost **Perpetual**, and (as the saying goes) it keeps no **Holidays**. <note>original: "Festos Dies." But shame arising from **Great Disgrace** that **Afflicts** for a long time contracts the **Spirits** even to the point of **Suffocation** and is destructive.

<summary>Bacon explains that constant envy is physically destructive, while the health impact of pity and shame depends on whether they trigger a lasting fear or a temporary contraction of the spirits.</summary>

_History of Life and Death._ <margin>251</margin>
***
**Love**, if it is not **Unhappy** and too **Wounding**, is a kind of **Joy**; and it is subject to the same **Laws** which we have set down concerning **Joy**. <margin>89</margin> <note>Bacon refers back to his earlier rules stating that moderate joy expands the spirits and warms the body, aiding longevity, whereas excessive or "unhappy" love would "wound" the spirit and consume it.</note>

**Hope** is the most useful of all **Affections**, and <margin>90</margin> contributes greatly to the prolongation of life; provided it does not fail too often, but feeds the **Imagination** with the **Contemplation of Good**. <note>original: "Boni Intuitu."</note> Therefore, those who fix and propose some **End** as the **Goal** of their life, and who make progress toward their **Desire** <note>original: "Voto."</note> both perpetually and gradually, are for the most part **Long-lived**. So much so that, when they reach the **Summit of their Hope** and no longer have anything further to hope for, they generally lose heart and do not survive for long. Thus, **Hope** seems to be a kind of **Leafed Joy**, which is stretched out to an immense extent, just like **Gold**. <note>original: "Gaudium Foliatum." This is a beautiful alchemical metaphor: just as a small amount of gold can be beaten into an incredibly thin, wide sheet of gold leaf, hope takes a small amount of actual pleasure and stretches it out to cover a whole lifetime.</note>
***
          R          **Admira-** <note>This "catchword" at the bottom of the page indicates the first word of the next page is "Admiratio" or "Admiration."</note>

<summary>Bacon characterizes love as a form of joy and identifies hope as the most beneficial emotion for longevity, comparing it to gold leaf that extends the spirit's vitality over a long duration.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Hope, Joy, Goal of life, Long-lived, Leafed Joy, Gold leaf, Affections, Vital spirits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s analysis of how different mental states and intellectual pursuits affect the "vital spirits." Having just discussed "Hope," he now transitions to the effects of wonder and light-hearted study on longevity.</meta>

<margin>91</margin>

# History of Life and Death.

**Admiration**, and **Light Contemplation**, contribute most greatly to the **prolongation of life**; <note>original: "Admiratio, & Leuis Contemplatio." In Bacon's philosophy, "Admiration" is the sense of wonder or surprise at the works of nature, which keeps the mind active without exhausting it.</note> for they keep the **Spirits** occupied with things that please them, and do not allow them to be disturbed, or to act in a restless and morose manner. Therefore, all those who were **Contemplators of Natural Things**, who had so many and such great wonders to admire (such as **Democritus**, **Plato**, **Parmenides**, and **Apollonius**), were long-lived. <note>Bacon lists philosophers known for their long lives: Democritus is said to have lived over 100 years; Apollonius of Tyana was a legendary sage also reputed to have reached a great age.</note> Likewise, the **Rhetoricians**, who merely "tasted" matters <note>original: "Res degustabant tantùm."</note> and pursued the **Light of Oratory** rather than the **Obscurity of Things**, were also long-lived; such as **Gorgias**, **Protagoras**, **Isocrates**, and **Seneca**. <note>Gorgias supposedly lived to 108. Bacon suggests that because these men focused on the beauty of speech (the "Light of Oratory") rather than the grueling, deep labor of uncovering difficult truths, their spirits remained youthful.</note> And certainly, just as old men are for the most part **Garrulous** and **Talkative**, so also do those who are talkative very often reach **Old Age**: for it indicates a **Light Contem-** <note>This "catchword" at the bottom of the page indicates the next page begins with the word "Contemplationem," continuing the thought that talkativeness reflects a specific, life-extending state of the vital spirits.</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that a sense of wonder and the "light" study of nature or rhetoric preserve the vital spirits by keeping them pleasantly occupied, citing various long-lived ancient philosophers and orators as proof.</summary>
<keywords>Admiration, Contemplation, Vital Spirits, Longevity, Democritus, Plato, Rhetoric, Seneca, Old Age, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

**Contemplation**, and one which does not greatly constrain or vex the **Spirit**; <note>The text continues from the previous page, finishing the thought that talkative people live long because their speech reflects a "light contemplation" of the world rather than a heavy, brooding one.</note> but **Subtle, Acute, and Sharp Inquiry** shortens life, for it tires and wears away the **Spirit**. <note>Bacon distinguishes between "light" study, which is refreshing, and "acute" study—deep, difficult research—which he believes physically consumes the vital spirits.</note>

In the physiology of the time, this refers to "insensible perspiration," the process by which internal vapors and spirits escape through the skin's pores.</note>, detains the **Inclosed Spirit** and turns it toward the **Harder Parts of the Body**; whereby the **Spirit** softens and tenderizes them.

this refers to *Mountains* where the *Air* is *Limpid* and pure; namely, where because of the *Aridity of the Valleys*, *Mists* and *Vapors* do not ascend; which occurs in the *Mountains* that surround *Barbary* <note>original: "Barbariam." 6 | And this kind of *Air*, whether of *Caves* or of *Mountains*, by its own natural property, preys <term>preys: Bacon uses the word "deprædatur," suggesting that common air acts like a predator, "stealing" or consuming the body’s vital moisture</term> little or nothing upon the body. But *Air*, such as our own, since it is *Predatory* due to the *Heat of the Sun*, must be excluded from the body as much as possible.

7 | *Air* indeed is prevented and excluded in two ways: first, if the *Passages* <term>Passages: Latin "Meatus," referring to the pores and channels of the skin and body</term> are *Closed*; second, if they are *Filled up* <note>original: "Oppleantur." This implies plugging or obstructing the pores, likely through the use of topical treatments or dietary changes to thicken the skin.</note>.

8 | To the *Closing of the Passages* <term>Passages: Latin "Meatus," referring to the pores and channels of the skin through which vital spirits might escape and external air might enter</term> the following contribute: the *Coldness of the Air* itself; *Nudity of the Skin*, by which it is hardened; *Washing in Cold Water*; and *Astringents* applied to the *Skin*, such as *Mastic*, *Myrrh*, and *Myrtle*.

...how he had preserved himself; he is said to have replied, *On the outside, with Oil; on the inside, with Honey.* <note>original: "Extrà, Oleo; intùs, Melle." This famous aphorism is often attributed to Democritus or Pollio Romulus.</note>

The *Irish*, especially the *Wild Irish* <note>original: "Syluestres." Bacon refers to the rural, Gaelic Irish who lived outside the English-controlled Pale.</note>, <margin>14</margin> are even to this day very long-lived. Indeed, it is said that a few years ago, the *Countess of Desmond* <note>Katherine FitzGerald, Countess of Desmond (d. 1604), who was a celebrated example of longevity in the Tudor and Stuart periods.</note> lived to her hundred-and-fortieth year, and that she *grew a new set of teeth* three times during her life. Furthermore, it is a custom among the *Irish* to rub themselves, while naked before a fire, with *Old Salted Butter*, as if they were curing or pickling themselves.

These same *Irish* also made use of *Linen* and *Shirts dyed with Saffron*; <margin>15</margin> and although this practice was introduced to *ward off decay* <note>original: "Arcendam Putrefactionem." Saffron was used in clothing primarily to prevent lice and keep the garments cleaner for longer in damp environments.</note>, we nevertheless believe it was (in some way) useful for *Length of Life*. For *Saffron*, out of all the substances I have known...

270 | *History of Life & Death.*

... [we] know, for the Skin and the Strengthening of the Flesh, is the best thing; since it is notably astringent <note>original: "astringat." In Bacon's physiological theory, astringent substances help "bind" the body's tissues, preventing the vital spirits from escaping.</note> and possesses, moreover, an oiliness and a subtle heat without any sharpness.

Indeed, I remember a certain *Englishman* who, to avoid paying customs duties, carried a bag of Saffron against his stomach while crossing the sea to keep it hidden. Although he had previously always suffered severely from seasickness, he remained in excellent health on that occasion and felt no nausea at all.

<margin>16</margin> *Hippocrates* <note>The famous Greek physician (c. 460 – c. 370 BC), often cited by Bacon as an authority on health.</note> commands that one should wear clean clothes against the skin in *Winter*, but in *Summer* they should be soiled and soaked in oil. The reason for this seems to be that during the *Summer*, the spirits <note>Bacon believed the body was animated by "vital spirits," subtle substances that could evaporate through the pores; their preservation was key to longevity.</note> exhale most heavily; therefore, the pores of the skin must be filled.

<margin>17</margin> Above all, therefore, we judge that the use of **Oil**, whether of **Olives** or of **sweet Almonds**, to anoint the **Skin** from the outside, contributes to **Longevity**; and this anointing ought to be done every **Dawn**, when rising from **Bed**, with an **Oil** in which a little **black Salt** <note>original: "Salis nigri." In early modern medicine, "black salt" often referred to unrefined sea salt or specific mineral preparations thought to have more "biting" or preservative properties than common white salt.</note> and **Saffron** <note>original: "Croci"</note> has been mixed. However, the anointing should be light, performed with **Wool** or a **soft Sponge**, so that it does not drip upon the **Body**, but only moistens and coats the **Skin**.

<margin>18</margin> It is certain that **Liquids** in a **Large Quantity**—even oily ones—draw something out from the **Body**; but on the contrary, in a **Small Quantity**, they are absorbed by the **Body**. Therefore, a light sprinkling should be performed, as we have said; or indeed, the **undershirt** <note>original: "Indusium." This refers to the linen garment worn closest to the skin, which Bacon suggests treating with oil to ensure constant, gentle contact with the body.</note> itself should be smeared with the **Oil**.

<margin>19</margin> It may perhaps be objected that this **Anointing** with **Oil** which we praise—though among us it has never been in use, and among the **Italians** it has fallen out of fashion—was once common among the **Greeks** and **Romans**, and part of their regular **Regimen** <note>original: "Diætæ." In early modern medicine, a "diet" or "regimen" referred to the entire daily routine, including food, exercise, and hygiene.</note>; and yet, in those ages, men were not notably longer-lived. 

But it is very rightly answered: **Oil** was only in use after **Baths**, except perhaps among **Athletes**; moreover, **Hot Baths** are as contrary to our **Operation** <note>original: "Operationi." Bacon uses this term to refer to his specific therapeutic goal: the preservation of the body's moisture and vital spirits.</note> as **Anointings** are suitable to it; since the one opens the **Pores** <note>original: "Meatus." Literally "passages," referring to the openings in the skin through which vapors escape.</note>, while the other obstructs them. 

Therefore, a **Bath** without a following **Anointing** is the worst; an **Anointing** without a **Bath** is the best. Furthermore, it was employed rather for **Luxury** <note>original: "Delicias." This suggests the ancients used oil for comfort and sensory pleasure rather than for the methodical preservation of the body.</note> and was rather applied—

<summary>Bacon argues that the ancient Greeks and Romans did not achieve longevity despite using oil because they combined it with hot baths, which open the pores and waste the body's spirits.</summary>

<margin>22</margin> The **first Inconvenience** is, that by suppressing **Sweats**, it may bring on **Diseases**, arising from those **Excrementitious Humors** <note>original: "Humoribus illis Excrementitijs." In early modern medicine, these were waste products or toxins that the body normally expelled through the pores to maintain balance.</note>: For this, a **Remedy** must be applied by way of **Purgations** and **Enemas** <note>original: "Clysterijs." Fluid injected into the lower bowel to stimulate evacuation.</note>, so that **Evacuation** is properly provided for; For it is certain that **Evacuation** through **Sweats** generally contributes to **Health**, but it hinders **Length of Life**. However, moderate **Purgatives** act upon the **Humors**, not upon the **Spirits** <note>original: "Spiritus." The vital, airy substances Bacon believed were responsible for life; he argued that while sweating expels waste, it also wastefully draws out these vital spirits, leading to aging.</note>, as **Sweating** does.

<margin>23</margin> The **second Inconvenience** is, that it may **heat the Body**, and from time to time

# OPERATION
# regarding the Blood, and the
# Heat that generates
# blood. III.

### *History.*

The two following **Operations** are to the two preceding **Operations** like **Antistrophes** <note>original: "Antistrophæ." In ancient Greek drama, the antistrophe was the returning movement of the chorus; here, Bacon uses it to mean a reciprocal counterpart or mirror image.</note>; and they correspond to them just as **Passives** relate to **Actives**. For the two preceding operations act to ensure that the **Spirits** and the **Air** are less **predatory** <note>original: "deprædantes." A central theme in Bacon's work: he believes that both the internal vital spirits and the external atmosphere act as "predators" that consume the body's moisture, leading to aging and death.</note> in their actions; these operations, however, ensure that the **Blood** and the **Juice** <note>original: "Succus." The vital fluids or "humors" that nourish the organs and keep the body supple.</note> of the **Body** are less subject to being preyed upon <note>The Latin text "depræ-" ends mid-word at the bottom of the page, continuing as "deprædabilis" on the following page.</note>.

<summary>Bacon introduces his third operation for longevity, which shifts the focus from external air to the internal quality of the blood. He explains that while previous steps limited the "hunger" of the air and spirits, this step aims to make the body's fluids harder to consume.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s investigation into the use of "hard" substances like gold and pearls to reinforce the blood. *History of Life and Death.* | 285

Since the **Arabs**, the **Greeks**, and the **moderns** have attributed such great **virtues** <note>original: "Virtutes." In the context of early modern medicine, this refers to the inherent power or healing efficacy of a substance.</note> to these things, it would seem there is not entirely nothing in those things which so many **experienced men** have observed. Therefore, setting aside the **fantastical opinions** concerning them, we clearly judge that if something can be introduced into the **whole substance of the blood** in the **smallest particles** <note>original: "per Minima." Bacon refers to the concept of *minima naturalia*, the smallest physical units of a substance, suggesting that these materials must be ground fine enough to enter the microscopic parts of the blood.</note>, something upon which the **Spirit** and **Heat** <note>original: "Spiritus, & Calor." According to Bacon, the internal "spirit" is like a flame that consumes the body’s moisture; he seeks substances that this "flame" cannot burn.</note> can act little or not at all, this would altogether resist not only **putrefaction** <note>The biological rotting or decay of the humors.</note> but also **drying out** <note>original: "Arefactioni." A central theme in this work: the process by which the body's tissues become dry and brittle with age.</note>; and it would be most effective for **prolonging life**.

In this, however, several **cautions** must be applied: **First**, that an extremely **exact crushing** <note>original: "Comminutio." The mechanical process of grinding solids into a fine powder.</note> take place; **Second**, that **hard and solid** things of this kind be **free from every malignant quality**,

[Footer:]
T 2
[free from...] <note>Catchword "pertia" is the conclusion of the Latin "expertia" (meaning "free from" or "devoid of"), which begins the next page.</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that while alchemical claims are often "fantastical," finely ground minerals and gems might physically reinforce the blood against the drying effects of internal heat. He emphasizes that these substances must be ground into "minima" (tiny particles) and be non-toxic to be effective.</summary>
<keywords>gold filings, pearls, blood substance, arefaction, comminution, longevity, minima naturalia, internal heat</keywords>

*History of Life and Death.*    **293**

***

**Regarding Exercise**;    **12**
An **idle life** clearly renders the **flesh soft** and **prone to dissipation** <note>original: "Dissipabiles." In Bacon's theory of aging, if tissues are too soft, the internal "spirit" of the body can easily consume or evaporate them, leading to decay.</note>: but **robust exercise** (provided **excessive sweating** or **fatigue** are avoided) makes the flesh **hard and compact**. Also, exercise in **cold waters**, such as **swimming**, is very good; and generally, **exercise in the open air** <note>original: "sub Dio." Literally "under the sky."</note> is better than **under a roof**.

Regarding **Rubbing** <note>original: "Fricationibus." This refers to massage or the friction of the skin with a cloth or brush, a common medical practice in the 17th century to stimulate the body's "spirits."</note> (which is a **type of exercise**); however, because it **draws forth nourishment** more than it **hardens** the flesh, we shall inquire into this later in its proper place.    **13**

Now indeed, since we have spoken concerning the **Hardness of the Juices**, we must come to their **Oiliness** or **Dewy State** <term>Dewy state (Roscidation): A term used by Bacon to describe a healthy, moist, and youthful condition of the body's fluids, preventing them from becoming dry or "parched" by age.</term> of those same—    **14**

<summary>Bacon explains how robust outdoor exercise and cold-water swimming harden the body's tissues against decay, then introduces the importance of maintaining oily or "dewy" bodily juices.</summary>
<keywords>exercise, swimming, open air, massage, dissipation of flesh, dewy state, bodily juices, Francis Bacon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Operation upon the Juices of the Body." It picks up from the end of page 293, completing a sentence regarding the transition from "Hardening" the body's tissues to the "Dewy State" or "Roscidation" of the juices.</meta>

294 | *History of Life and Death.*
____________________________________
of those juices themselves; which is a more perfect and powerful **Goal** <term>Intention (Intentio): In Bacon’s natural philosophy, an "intention" refers to a specific medical objective or a directed course of physical action intended to produce a certain effect on the body.</term> than **Hardening**; because it does not have the **Disadvantage** or **Evil** entangled with it. For all things that pertain to the **Hardness of the Juices** are of such a nature that, while they prevent the **Consumption of Nourishment** <note>original: "Alimenti Absumptionem." This refers to the process where the body’s innate heat "consumes" or dries up its own fuel and moisture.</note>, they also hinder the **Repair** of that same nourishment; from which it follows that for the **Prolongation of Life**, these same things both help and hinder. But those things which pertain to the **Dewiness of the Juices** <term>Dewiness (Roscidation): A specialized term Bacon uses to describe making the body's fluids oily, moist, and "dew-like" to prevent them from being easily evaporated or dried out by age.</term> help in both respects; since they render the **Nourishment** both less **Prone to Dissipation** <note>original: "Dissipabile." Meaning the juices are less likely to be scattered or wasted by the body's internal heat.</note> and more **Capable of Repair**.

**15** When we say, however, that the **Juice of the Body** ought to become **Dewy** and **Oily**, it must be noted that we do not understand this to refer to **Fatness** <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; Bacon is beginning to distinguish between healthy, functional "dewiness" and the mere accumulation of body fat.</note>—

<summary>Bacon argues that while hardening bodily tissues prevents waste, it also blocks nourishment; however, making juices "dewy" or oily is a superior strategy because it simultaneously preserves existing moisture and allows for easier bodily repair.</summary>
<keywords>roscidation, dewiness, bodily juices, nourishment, prolongation of life, dissipation, repair, Francis Bacon</keywords>
