...fat, but of a **Dew** <note>original: "Rore." Bacon uses "Dew" to describe a subtle, pervasive moisture rather than gross fat.</note> spread throughout, and (if you like) a **Radical Moisture** <term>Radical Moisture: A traditional medical concept referring to the innate fluid of an organism that sustains life; when it is consumed, the organism dies.</term>, within the very **Substance of the Body**.

**16** Nor again should anyone think that the **Oil**, or the **Fats of Foods**, or **Marrows**, generate their like and satisfy our **Objective** <term>Objective (Intention): The specific medical goal of making body juices oily and dewy to prevent decay.</term>; for things that have been once perfected do not turn back <note>Bacon means that once a substance is already "perfected" as fat or oil outside the body, it cannot be easily re-processed by the body into the specific "radical" moisture needed for longevity.</note>; but such foods ought to be those which, after **Digestion** and **Ripening** <note>original: "Maturationem." This refers to the secondary stage of digestion where nutrients are refined into usable substances for the body.</note>, only then produce an **Oiliness** in the juices.

**17** Nor again should anyone think that **Oil** and **Fat**, when concentrated and **Simple** <note>Meaning pure, unmixed oil or fat.</note>, are difficult to **Dissipate** <note>original: "Dissipationis." The process of the body's heat scattering or evaporating its fluids.</note>, yet do not retain that same nature when in a **Mixture**; for just as oil...

<summary>Bacon argues that longevity is achieved by making the body's internal juices "dewy" and "oily" through proper digestion, rather than by simply consuming fatty foods. He distinguishes between gross body fat and a refined "radical moisture" that resists being dried up by the body's heat.</summary>

*History of Life and Death.* 301

## OPERATION
## upon the Viscera for the Extrusion of Nourishment. V.

### History.

<margin>1</margin> Those things which strengthen the **Principal Viscera** <note>The major internal organs.</note> (which are the **Fountains of Concoction** <note>Bacon uses "concoction" to describe the series of stages—similar to a biological "cooking"—where the body transforms food into blood and then into solid tissue.</note>)—namely the **Stomach**, **Liver**, **Heart**, and **Brain**—to properly exercise their functions (whereby **Nourishments** are distributed to the various parts, the **Spirits** <note>In the medical theory of the time, "spirits" were subtle, air-like fluids that flowed through the body to govern movement and sensation.</note> are dispersed, and through which the **Repair** of the entire **Body** is accomplished) by **Physicians**,

V 2 <note>This "V 2" is a signature mark, a printer's tool used to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct order.</note> and

<summary>Bacon introduces his fifth "Operation" for longevity, focusing on how the primary organs (stomach, liver, heart, and brain) must be strengthened to ensure that nutrition is effectively distributed throughout the body. He identifies these organs as the "fountains" of digestion and physical repair.</summary>

  5      We wonder how that practice of **Drinking Warm Liquids** (which was in use among the **Ancients**) has fallen into **Disuse**; We certainly knew a very famous physician who, at **Lunch** and **Dinner**, used to greedily swallow **Broth** <margin>+</margin> that was even **scalding hot**, and a little later wished for it to be brought back up <note>That is, he wished to vomit the liquid back out once the heat had been transferred to his stomach.</note>; for he said, "I have no need for the **Broth**, but only for the **Heat**."

  6      We consider it entirely beneficial that the first **Drink**, whether of **Wine**, or **Beer**, or any other **Beverage** (whichever one is accustomed to), should always be served **warm** at **Dinner**.

  7      We consider **Wine of the Quenching of Gold** <note>This refers to a common early modern medical preparation where red-hot gold was dipped into wine. It was believed the "spirit" or "virtue" of the metal was transferred to the liquid.</note> to be useful once during a **Meal**; Not because we believe that **Gold** bestows any special **Virtue** <note>original: *Virtutis*. In this context, it refers to a quasi-magical or medicinal power.</note> for this purpose, but because we know that every **Metallic Quenching** in any **Liquid** imparts a powerful **Constriction** <term>Astrictionem: A "tightening" or "toning" effect on the stomach's tissues, intended to prevent the stomach from becoming too relaxed or "leaky."</term>: we choose **Gold**, however, because beyond that **Constriction** (which we desire), it leaves behind no other **Metallic Impression** <note>original: *Metallicæ Impressionis*. Bacon is being scientific here: he wants the physical effect of the metal-cooling process, but chooses gold because it is chemically inert and won't leave behind a toxic or unwanted medicinal "taint" like lead or copper might.</note>.

  8      We judge **Sops of Bread in Wine** <note>original: *Offas Panis*. Small pieces or morsels of bread soaked in wine, a common restorative in the 17th century.</note>, taken in the **middle of a Meal**, to be more useful than the **Wine** itself; especially...

[space] V 4 [space] fi- <note>Catchword for the next page, likely leading into a word such as "firmare" (to strengthen).</note>

is required: For that **dissolution** <note>original: *Exolutio*. **19** To this subject belong especially those things which were described in the **Operation upon the Blood** <note>Bacon’s "Operations" are specific therapeutic chapters in this book. The "Operation upon the Blood" (Operation 5) deals with cooling and densifying the blood to prevent it from consuming the body's tissues.</note>; to these we shall add only a very few, but select, points.

**20** Most importantly, let **Wine of Sweet Pomegranates** be used; or if that cannot be had, their **freshly squeezed Juice**. It should be taken in the **Morning** with some **Sugar**, having placed into the **Glass** (into which the juice is squeezed) a small amount of fresh **Lemon Peel** and three or four whole **Cloves**. This should be used from **February** until the **end of April**.

Many of those things which were said above concerning the **Spirits** <note>original: *Spiriti-bus*. For it is certain that just as **Pestilential Airs** are found which do not stink as much as others that are less harmful; so likewise, on the contrary, **Very Healthy Airs** are found, and those **Most Friendly to the Spirits**, which are either completely odorless or less **Pleasant** and **Fragrant** to the senses. <margin>31</margin> We praise above all others (as we also suggested above) **Odors from Living Plants** that have not been **Plucked**, but are instead received in the **Open Air**. Therefore, **Walking** or **Sitting** amidst breezes of this kind should be a frequent practice.

<margin>32</margin> For the **benefit of the Heart**, we prefer **Cooling Odors** over those that are warmer; therefore, a **Morning Fumigation** <note>original: *suffitus*. The practice of inhaling or being surrounded by medicinal vapors or smoke.</note>, or one performed during the **Heat of Midday**, is best. This should be made of equal portions of **Vinegar**, **Rose Water**, and a **Generous Wine**, poured over a **Plate of Iron** heated until it is nearly glowing.

<margin>33</margin> Nor should anyone think that we are offering a **libation** <note>A ritual pouring of liquid as an offering to a deity.</note> to **Mother Earth** if we prescribe that, during **Digging** or the **turning of the Soil**, a **Generous Wine** should be poured upon it. <note>Bacon frequently argued that the "breath" of the earth was life-prolonging; here he suggests wine enhances the restorative vapors released when the ground is disturbed.</note>

40 <margin>Regarding the **Brain** (where the **Throne** and **Academy** <note>original: *Cathedra & Universitas*, implying the brain is both the seat of authority and the gathering place for these forces</note> of the **Animal Spirits** <term>animal spirits: in 17th-century physiology, these were highly refined vapors believed to carry out the functions of thought, sensation, and movement</term> reside), those matters which were investigated earlier—concerning **Opium**, **Nitre** <note>saltpeter, which Bacon believed could cool and condense the spirits to preserve life</note>, and their related substances, and also the **Procurement of Peaceful Sleep**—pertain to this section as well to some extent. <summary>Bacon recommends a bi-weekly medicinal broth to invigorate the brain and spirit, while emphasizing that a simple, high-quality diet is more effective for the major organs than a complex variety of medicines.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Sixth Operation" concerning the attraction of nourishment by the external parts of the body. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, completing the discussion on how internal and external faculties must work in tandem.</meta>

*History of Life and Death.* [Page number] 327

-ment <note>original: *mentationem*; this completes the word *Alimentationem* (nourishment) from the previous page</note> holds the primary role; nevertheless, the **Actions of the External Parts** must also concur; so that just as the **Internal Faculty** emits and extrudes the **Aliment** <term>Aliment: nourishment or food processed for the body's use</term>, so the **Faculty of the External Parts** should seize and attract the same. And the weaker the **Faculty of Concoction** <term>Concoction: the process by which the body "cooks" or transforms food into blood and tissue</term> is, the more need there is for the concurring help of the **Attractive Faculty**.

<margin>2</margin> A strong **Attraction of the External Parts** is excited chiefly by **Bodily Motion**, through which the **Parts**, being **warmed and strengthened**, more eagerly call and attract the **Aliment** to themselves.

<margin>3</margin> But this must be especially guarded against and forbidden: lest that same **Motion** and **Heat** which calls the **New Juice** <note>original: *Succum*; the nutrient-rich fluid or "sap" extracted from food to build the body</note> to the **Limbs**, should at the same time [consume?] the **Limb** <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence and will continue on the following page.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that while internal digestion is vital, the outer limbs must actively "pull" nourishment toward themselves, a process aided by the heat of physical exercise. However, he warns that the same exercise must not be so intense that it consumes the body's resources faster than it attracts them.</summary>
<keywords>external parts, attraction of nourishment, bodily motion, concoction, aliment, vital heat, Francis Bacon</keywords>

the open air <note>original: *Sub Dio*</note> rather than under a **Roof**, and in **Winter** rather than **Summer**; And furthermore, **Exercise** should not only be concluded with an **Anointing** <term>Anointing: the application of oil (Inunctio) to seal the pores of the skin</term>, as is the case with **Friction**; but even in more vigorous **Exercises**, **Anointing** should be applied both at the beginning and toward the end, in the manner of the **Athletes**.

**4** | Since we advised a little earlier that the first **Drink** at dinner should be taken **warm**; we now add that for the **Preparation of the Stomach**, even a **half-hour before food**, a **generous draught** of drink (whatever one is most accustomed to) should be taken **warm**, but only **slightly spiced** for the sake of flavor. <note>Bacon believes that warming the stomach before a meal "awakens" its digestive heat, making it more efficient at processing the coming food.</note>
**5** | The **Preparation of Foods**, and of **Bread**, and of **Drinks**, if established well and in accordance with our **Intention** <note>In Bacon’s system, "Intention" refers to a specific scientific goal—in this case, the preservation of the body's vital spirits.</note>, is truly of **great importance**. Although it is a **mechanical matter** <note>original: *Res Mechanica*; Bacon uses this to mean manual labor or craft, often looked down upon by the philosophers of his time.</note> and savors of the **Kitchen** and the **Cellar**, it nevertheless far excels the **fables about Gold**, and **Gems**, and such things. <note>Bacon is critiquing "spagyric" or alchemical medicines that claimed drinking dissolved gold or crushed jewels would grant immortality; he prefers practical, biological interventions.</note>
**6** | The **Moistening of the Body's Juices** through the **moist preparation of food** is a **childish thing**; <note>He suggests that merely eating "wet" or "soupy" foods is a naive way to hydrate the internal organs compared to the more complex chemical methods he proposes.</note> toward the **Heats**

<margin>1</margin>
It is certain that **all bodies** are endowed with a certain desire to **assimilate** <note>original: *Assimilandi*. In Bacon's natural philosophy, "assimilation" is the universal tendency of a substance to turn neighboring substances into its own likeness.</note> those things which are nearby. **Thin** and **pneumatic** <term>pneumatic: relating to "spirits" or gas-like substances that Bacon believed resided within all matter, driving change and movement</term> substances—such as flame, spirit, and air—perform this generously and eagerly. On the other hand, those things which possess a **thick** and **tangible mass** do so quite weakly; this is because that desire for assimilation is held back by a stronger desire for **rest** <note>original: *Quie-tis*. This refers to inertia or the tendency of solid matter to remain stable and resistant to change.</note>,

<summary>Bacon begins his commentary on assimilation by explaining that while all matter seeks to transform neighboring substances into its own likeness, active "pneumatic" substances like air and flame do so much more effectively than solid matter.</summary>

# The Porches of Death

**C**oncerning the Porches of Death <margin>To be investigated according to the article; that is, Article 15.</margin> <note>original: *Atriola Mortis*. This architectural metaphor refers to the "vestibules" or entryways of death—the immediate physiological signs and thresholds that precede the end of life.</note> that is, concerning those things which happen to the dying in the very moment <margin>Connection</margin> <note>original: *Connexio*. A transitional note linking the previous scientific observations to the specific moment of passing.</note> of Death, and a little Before, and After: So that, since Death is reached by many different paths, it may be understood into what common traits those paths dissolve; especially in those Deaths which are brought on by the exhaustion of Nature, rather than 
                                  than <note>The word "rather than" (potiùs quàm) is split across the page break, with "than" intended to begin the next page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the "Porches of Death," a study of the common physiological symptoms and states that occur immediately before, during, and after the moment of passing.</summary>
<keywords>Porches of Death, Atriola Mortis, physiology of dying, natural death, clinical observation, Francis Bacon</keywords>

372 | *History of Life & Death.*
---
than by Violence; although even some of these things, on account of the Connection of Things <note>original: *Rerum Connexionem*. This refers to the philosophical idea that all natural phenomena are interlinked, meaning observations from violent deaths can still inform our understanding of natural ones.</note>, must be interspersed <note>original: *inspergendum*. Literally "to sprinkle in," suggesting these observations are scattered throughout the text as relevant.</note>.

### *History.*

**1** The living Spirit <term>Spirit: In 17th-century physiology, "spirits" were not ghosts, but extremely fine, vaporous substances that traveled through the nerves to animate the body and facilitate thought.</term> seems to require three things to sustain itself: suitable Motion; tempered Cooling; and proper Nourishment. A flame, however, seems to require only two of these: namely, Motion and Nourishment; because a flame is a simple substance, whereas the Spirit is a Composite substance; so much so that if the Spirit passes a little too closely into a Flaming Nature, it destroys itself. <note>Bacon compares the "vital spirit" of a human to a flame, but notes a crucial difference: while a flame needs heat, the human spirit needs "cooling" (respiration) to keep its delicate composite balance from burning out.</note>

**2** Even a flame is dissolved and killed by a greater and more powerful flame. <note>Bacon is likely referring to the observation that a smaller candle flame can be "consumed" or extinguished when placed inside a much larger fire, a principle he applies to the interactions of spirits within the body.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the introduction to the study of death and begins a "History" (a collection of observations) comparing the vital spirit of living beings to the properties of flame, specifically noting the spirit's unique need for "cooling."</summary>
<keywords>vital spirit, physiology of death, flame analogy, cooling/refrigerio, Francis Bacon, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 383 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered "History" or experimental observations from page 372. It concludes point 2 regarding the interaction of flames and begins a sequence of observations (3-5) regarding how compression and the lack of space extinguish both physical fire and the vital spirit of the body.</meta>

[Header]
History of Life & Death. | 373
[End Header]

is killed, as Aristotle <note>original: *Aristoteles*. Francis Bacon frequently cites Aristotle’s works on natural philosophy, such as *On Respiration*, though he often sought to improve upon Aristotle’s conclusions through his own experimental method.</note> well noted; much more so the Spirit.

**3** A flame, if it is compressed too much, is extinguished; as may be seen in a candle when a glass is placed over it: For the air, dilated by heat, crowds the flame, and diminishes and extinguishes it. Nor is a flame ignited in hearths <note>original: *Caminis*. Refers to chimneys or fireplaces.</note> if the fuel is packed together too tightly without some space left between.

**4** Even ignited things are extinguished by compression; as if you strongly compress a glowing coal with iron or with your foot, the fire is immediately extinguished.

**5** But to come to the Spirit; if blood or phlegm <term>Phlegm: In the medical theory of the "Four Humors," phlegm was the cold, moist fluid. Here, Bacon uses it to describe any thick, obstructive bodily fluid.</term> rushes into the ventricles <note>original: *ventriculos*. These are the cavities within the brain where the "animal spirits" were thought to be produced and stored.</note> of the brain, death occurs suddenly;

<summary>Bacon compares the vital spirit to a flame, noting that both are extinguished by excessive compression or a lack of space, and applies this to how sudden death occurs when fluids block the brain.</summary>

<margin>10</margin> Extreme grief and fear, especially when sudden (as happens with bad and unforeseen news), sometimes result in sudden death.

<margin>11</margin> But it is not only excessive compression, but also excessive dilation of the spirit, that is fatal.

<margin>12</margin> Great and sudden joys have robbed many of their lives. <note>Bacon often cited historical examples, such as the Roman matron who died of joy upon seeing her son return from the Battle of Cannae, to illustrate how the spirit could "rush outward" too quickly.</note>

Bacon argues that when fluid leaves the body too quickly, the vital spirit rushes out to fill the empty space, leaving the organs lifeless.</note>

The Motion of the Heart, namely its Systole and Diastole <note>These terms refer to the rhythmic contraction (*systole*) and expansion (*diastole*) of the heart. Bacon viewed this mechanical motion as the primary driver of the body’s "vital spirit."</note>, is three times faster than Respiration; so much so that if it were possible for that Motion in the Heart to be stopped, even without inhibiting Respiration, Death would follow even more quickly than from Strangulation.

**The Proportion of Nourishment.**
**28** | Let us calculate the accounts of our bodily needs, according to the ordinary and accustomed course of Nature. The **Spirit** <note>original: *Spiritus*; for Bacon, this is a thin, invisible substance—halfway between air and flame—that resides in the body and powers all functions</note> perpetually requires the unfolding of its own motion within the ventricles of the brain and the nerves; it requires the motion of the heart every third part of a moment; breathing every single moment; sleep and food within a three-day period; and the very capacity for being nourished [fails] after approximately eighty years. And if any of these needs are not met, Death follows. Thus, there clearly appear to be three *Outer Courts of Death* <note>original: *Atriola Mortis*; literally "little halls" or entryways. Bacon envisions death not as a single event but as a process of entering these final chambers</note>: the failure of the Spirit in its motion; in its cooling <note>Bacon believed the primary purpose of breathing was to cool the "vital heat" of the spirit so it wouldn't consume the body too quickly</note>; and in its nourishment.

29 | There are two great *Precursors of Death*; one sent from the *Head*, the other from the *Heart*: *Convulsion*, and the *Extreme Struggle of the Pulse*; for even that *deadly hiccup* <note>original: *singultus*; a gasping or sobbing breath often seen in the dying</note> is a type of *Convulsion*. Stiffness of the Nerves and Parts; the Loss of all Heat; and a little while after, Putrefaction and Stench.

**32** | Eels, Serpents, and Insects move for a long time in their individual parts after being cut up; so much so that country folk <note>original: *rustici*; Bacon often records folk beliefs as a starting point for scientific inquiry.</note> believe the individual parts are striving to unite with one another again. Even birds, once their heads are torn off, flutter for a time; indeed, the hearts of animals continue to throb for a long time after being removed. I myself certainly remember seeing the heart of a man who had been eviscerated (a type of punishment used among us for traitors <note>Bacon refers to the sentence for High Treason in England: being hanged, drawn, and quartered. This often involved the removal of the heart while the victim was still technically alive.</note>), which, having been thrown into the fire according to custom, leaped upwards—at first to the height of a foot and a half <note>original: *sesquipedem*; a Roman measurement of 1.5 feet.</note>, and then gradually less; for the duration (as I recall) of seven, or

397

# History of Life & Death.

To reach full growth; then the state of peak strength in the limbs, and also of agility; to turn gray-haired, and to go bald; the cessation of the menstrual flow, and of the power of procreation; to decline toward decrepitude, and become a <term>three-legged animal<note>original: *Animal Tripes*; a classical reference to the Riddle of the Sphinx, describing an elderly person who must use a cane as a "third leg."</note></term>; and finally, to die. Meanwhile, the mind also has its own periods and stages, but these cannot be so easily described by specific years: such as a failing memory and similar failings, which we shall discuss later.

<margin>2</margin> The distinctions between **Youth** and **Old Age** are these: The skin of the young is smooth and stretched even; in the old it is dry and wrinkled, especially around the forehead and eyes. The flesh of the young is tender and soft; in the old it is harder. Strength and agility belong to the young; in the old there is a diminishing of forces and a slowness of movement. In the young, there is a <term>vigor of the digestions<note>original: *Coctionum Validitas*; "coction" was the contemporary term for the body's internal "cooking" or processing of food and humors.</note></term>; in the old,

--- Page 408 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the bodies of the young and the old, picking up from the catchword on page 397 to provide a detailed list of physiological differences.</meta>

398 | History of Life & Death.
***

**Weakness**: In the young, the internal organs are soft and succulent; in the old, they are salty and shriveled. In the young, the body is more erect; in the old, there is a leaning toward a curve. In the young, the limbs are steady; in the old, there is weakness and trembling. In the young, the <term>humors<note>original: *humores*; the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to determine health and temperament.</note></term> are bilious and the blood is more heated; in the old, the humors are phlegmatic and melancholic, and the blood is colder. 

In the young, <term>Venus<note>original: *Venus*; the Roman goddess of love, used here as a metaphor for sexual desire and reproductive power.</note></term> is ready at hand; in the old, she is slower. In the young, the juices of the body are more <term>dewy<note>original: *roscidi*; Bacon used this term to describe the life-sustaining, balm-like moisture that keeps the body youthful.</note></term>; in the old, they are more raw and watery. 

In the young, the <term>spirit<note>original: *spiritus*; the "vital spirits" were thought to be an invisible, air-like substance that acted as the bridge between the soul and the body, driving all movement and thought.</note></term> is plentiful and swelling; in the old, it is scant and meager. In the young, the spirit is dense and fresh; in the old, it is sharp and thin. In the young, the senses are lively and intact; in the old, they are duller and failing. In the young, the teeth are strong and whole; in the old, they are weak, worn down, and falling out.

History of Life and Death. | 399
________________________________________

In the young, the hair is colored; in the old, no matter what the original color might have been, it turns gray. In the young, there is a full head of hair; in the old, baldness. In the young, the pulse is stronger and more rapid; in the old, it is fainter and slower. In the young, diseases are more acute and curable; in the old, they are more chronic and difficult to treat. In the young, wounds knit together more quickly; in the old, more slowly. In the young, the cheeks bloom with color; in the old, they are either pale or ruddy, and with thick blood. In the young, there is less discomfort from <term>catarrhs<note>original: *catarrhis*; an excessive buildup of mucus in the nose or throat, often associated in early medicine with an imbalance of the "cold" and "moist" humors.</note></term>; in the old, it is greater.

Nor do we know of anything in which the old improve (at least regarding the body), except perhaps occasionally in obesity. The cause for this is readily apparent: the bodies of the old neither <term>perspire<note>original: *perspirant*; in Bacon’s physiology, this refers to "insensible perspiration," the release of invisible vapors through the skin, which was considered vital for maintaining the body's internal balance.</note></term> well, nor do they <term>assimilate<note>original: *assimilant*; the metabolic process by which the body converts food and nutrients into its own living tissue.</note></term> well. Fatness, moreover, is nothing other than an exuberance—
            Cc 3      —ance <note>This catchword "berantia" completes the word "Exuberantia" (exuberance or overflow) from the previous line.</note>

Such explanations are of no practical use. This much is certain: in the course of aging, dryness precedes coldness; while bodies are in their highest state and peak of heat, they decline toward dryness, and coldness follows only afterward.

## **FLUID CANONS**
## on the **Duration of Life**,
## and the **Form of Death**.
<note>original: *Canones Mobiles*; Bacon uses the term "mobile" or "fluid" to indicate these are working axioms or provisional rules derived from his observations, rather than final, immutable dogmas.</note>
____________________________________

**CANON I.**

**Consumption** <note>original: *Consumptio*; in this context, the physical wearing away or gradual wasting of the body’s substance.</note> does not occur, unless that which is lost from the **Body** migrates into **Another Body**.

**EXPLANATION.**

There is no **Annihilation of Matter** <note>original: *Nullus est Rerum Interitus*; this is an early statement of the principle of the conservation of mass—the idea that matter cannot be destroyed, only transformed or moved.</note>: Therefore, that which is consumed either flies off into the **Air**, or is received...

<summary>This page introduces the formal "Canons" of the text, beginning with a fundamental physical law: that bodily decay is not the destruction of matter, but its transition into another form, such as air.</summary>
<keywords>Canons, Consumption, Conservation of Matter, Physics of Aging, Francis Bacon, Transformation of Substance</keywords>

--- Page 418 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Canon I, which posits that matter is never destroyed but only transferred. It concludes the first explanation and introduces Canon II, regarding the internal "spirit" of all matter.</meta>

408 | *History of Life and Death.*
--- | ---
...is received into some adjacent body:
Therefore we see a spider, a fly, or an ant, buried in amber—a monument more than royal—rendered eternal; even though they are delicate and easily dissolved things. Truly, no air is present into which any part might fly off; and the substance of the amber is so different in nature <note>original: *heterogenea*; in Bacon’s theory, for one substance to "consume" another, they must share some similarity; amber is too dissimilar to the biological matter of the insect to draw anything out of it</note> that it receives nothing from them. We believe the same would happen if wood, or a root, or something of that kind, were placed in quicksilver <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury, which Bacon often experimented with due to its high density and fluid nature</note>. But wax, honey, and gums have a similar effect, though only in part.

**CANON II.**

**In every tangible thing** there is a **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*; for Bacon, "spirit" is not a mystical or religious soul, but a very thin, invisible, and active physical substance that resides in the pores of all matter and acts as the primary agent of change and decay</note>, hidden by the **thicker body**...

<summary>Bacon explains that preservation occurs when a substance—like amber or mercury—prevents a body's internal matter from escaping into the air. He then introduces his second fundamental law: that all physical matter contains an internal "spirit" that drives its transformation.</summary>

<keywords>preservation, amber, mercury, quicksilver, spirit, tangible matter, Francis Bacon, physics of decay</keywords>

### EXPLANATION.

No body known to us, here on the Upper Part of the Earth, is without a spirit; whether through the thinning and ripening <term>concoction: a historical scientific term (original: *concoctio*) referring to the process where heat matures or transforms a substance</term> caused by the heat of the celestial bodies, or otherwise. For the cavities within tangible things do not admit a vacuum; instead, they contain either air or a spirit proper to the thing itself. This spirit (of which we speak) is not some abstract power, or "energy," or entelechy <term>entelechy: an Aristotelian term (original: *entelechia*) for a vital force that directs a thing toward its purpose; Bacon rejects this as too mystical</term>, or mere nonsense; it is plainly a thin, invisible body;
                                          but—

<summary>Bacon explains that all matter on Earth contains an internal "spirit" created by celestial heat or other forces. He insists this spirit is a physical, though invisible, substance rather than a mystical or abstract concept.</summary>

—nevertheless, it is something localized, measurable, and real. Nor, again, is that spirit <term>spirit: in Bacon’s physics, "spirit" (original: *spiritus*) refers to a subtle, gaseous matter trapped inside solid bodies that drives change, rather than a supernatural soul</term> air (just as the juice of a grape is not water), but a thin body, related to air, yet greatly different from it.

The denser parts of a substance (being of a sluggish nature and not very mobile) would naturally endure for long periods; but it is that spirit which agitates, pierces, and undermines them. It preys upon the moisture of the body and whatever else it can digest <note>original: *digerere*; here meaning to process or transform through internal heat</note>, transforming it into new spirit; then, both the spirit that was already within the body and the spirit that has been newly created gradually fly away together. This is best shown by the decrease in weight in bodies that have been dried out through perspiration <note>perspiration: in this context, the process of internal moisture and spirits evaporating out of a solid object as it withers or dries</note>. ### RULE III.

**T**he Spirit, when **Released**, dries things out; when **Detained** and working within, it either liquefies, or rots, or gives life.

### EXPLANATION.

**T**here are four processes of the Spirit: towards <term>Drying: (original: *Arefactionem*) the process of a substance becoming dry as the internal spirit escapes</term>; towards <term>Liquefaction: (original: *Colliquationem*) the process of a solid turning into a liquid state</term>; towards Putrefaction; towards Gene- <note>The word is cut off at the page break; it is the beginning of "Generation," or the creation of new life/form</note>

<summary>Bacon defines Rule III of his physical theory: if the internal "spirit" of an object escapes, the object dries out; if the spirit is trapped, it either melts the object, causes it to rot, or creates new life.</summary>

<keywords>spirit, drying, liquefaction, putrefaction, generation, physics, Francis Bacon, material transformation, decay</keywords>

408 | History of Life and Death.

ration of Bodies. <term>Drying: (original: *arefactio*) the process by which a body becomes parched or withered as its internal spirit departs</term> is not the proper work of the Spirit, but of the <term>Coarser Parts: (original: *partes crassiores*) the tangible, solid matter of a body that remains when the spirit leaves</term>, after the Spirit has been sent forth: for then those parts contract themselves, partly through a <term>Flight of the Vacuum: (original: *fuga vacui*) the traditional physical theory that nature "abhors a vacuum" and pulls matter together to ensure no empty space is left behind</term>, and partly through the union of like-natured parts: as is clear in all things that dry out by age; and in drier bodies that are desiccated by fire, such as bricks, coals, and bread.

For indeed, once the Spirit (which used to hold together and restrain the parts of the thing) is partly sent forth and partly weakened, all things are dissolved and return to their own <term>Heterogeneities: (original: *heterogenias*) substances of different kinds or "un-like" natures</term>, or—if you prefer—their own Elements. ## RULE 4. <note>original: *Canon*; Bacon uses "Canons" to establish fixed laws of nature in his scientific method.</note>

->In all living things there are two kinds of **Spirits**: **Mortal Spirits**, such as are found in inanimate objects; and a super-added **Vital Spirit**.<-

### EXPLANATION.

It has already been stated that, in order to achieve <term>Longevity: (original: *longæuitas*) the preservation and extension of life</term>, the human body must be considered in two ways: first, as if it were **Inanimate** and not receiving nourishment; and second, as an **Animate** object that is nourished.

Dd 3 <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct order.</note> For... <note>This "For" (original Latin: *Nam*) is a catchword, a common printing practice where the first word of the following page is printed at the bottom of the current one to ensure continuity.</note>

<summary>Bacon defines two types of "spirits" within living organisms: the "mortal" spirit shared with non-living matter and the "vital" spirit unique to life. He argues that understanding long life requires studying the body both as a physical object and a biological system.</summary>
<keywords>spirit, mortal spirit, vital spirit, longevity, inanimate, animate, Francis Bacon, biology</keywords>

For the first consideration <note>original: *Prior Consideratio*; referring to the view of the body as an inanimate object.</note> provides the laws concerning **Consumption**; the second, the laws concerning **Repair** <note>original: *Reparatione*; the process of the body restoring its lost parts through nourishment.</note>. Therefore, we must understand that within human **Flesh**, **Bones**, **Membranes**, **Organs**, and finally in every individual part while they are alive, there are certain **Spirits** <note>In 17th-century science, "spirits" were not ghosts, but extremely subtle, vaporous substances believed to permeate all matter and drive physical processes like heat and decay.</note> diffused throughout their substance. These are the same kinds of spirits found in such things—flesh, bone, and membrane—when they are separated and dead, and which remain even in a corpse. 

But the **Vital Spirit**, although it governs these others and shares a certain harmony with them, is far different from them; it is whole and exists independently. There are, however, two primary differences between **Mortal Spirits** and **Vital Spirits**. The first is that mortal spirits are not at all continuous within themselves, but are as if cut off— <note>The text ends mid-word (original: *abscis-*) as a catchword for the next page.</note>

### RULE V <note>original: *Canon V*; Bacon uses "Canons" as fundamental laws or principles of his scientific system.</note>

**Natural Actions** belong specifically to **individual Parts** of the body, but the **vital Spirit** excites and sharpens them.

EXPLA-

<summary>Bacon argues that the body's vital spirit is a gentle, airy form of fire, much milder than an alcohol flame. He establishes a new principle: while organs have their own natural functions, the vital spirit is the catalyst that activates and sharpens those actions.</summary>

### EXPLANATION

The **Actions** or **Functions** which exist in individual **Members** <note>original: *Membris*; in 17th-century anatomy, this refers to specific organs or functional parts of the body.</note> follow the **Nature** of those **Members** themselves; (**Attraction**, **Retention**, **Digestion**, **Assimilation**, **Separation**, **Excretion**, **Perspiration**, and even **Sensation** itself;) according to the **Property** of the individual **Organs** (the **Stomach**, **Liver**, **Heart**, **Spleen**, **Gall**, **Brain**, **Eye**, **Ear**, and the rest). And yet, not one of these **Actions** would ever be put into motion, were it not for the vigor and **Presence** of the **vital Spirit** and its **Heat**; just as **Iron** would not attract other **Iron** unless it were excited <note>Bacon is using the property of magnetism as an analogy: just as a piece of iron needs to be "excited" or magnetized to act upon another, the body's organs need the vital spirit to perform their natural biological roles.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that while every organ has a specific natural function (like digestion or sensation), these functions remain dormant without the "activation" provided by the vital spirit and its heat.</summary>
<keywords>physiology, organs, vital spirit, magnetism, biological functions, Francis Bacon, animation, heat</keywords>

But as for the Second Desire of Flying out,
and of taking itself into the Air; it is certain
that all Thin things (which are always Mobile)
are willingly carried toward their Like in the
Vicinity; as a Bubble of Water is carried to a
Bubble, Flame to Flame: But this happens
much more in the Flying out of the Spirit
into the Surrounding <note>The Latin text ends mid-word with *Ambien-*, which completes as *Ambiente* (surrounding).</note> Air.

<summary>Bacon explains how inanimate spirits trapped within solid matter "prey" on their surroundings to multiply themselves and naturally seek to escape into the open air to join similar substances.</summary>

### RULE VIII.

**A** Spirit that is held back, if it does not have the opportunity to generate another spirit, also softens the coarser parts <note>original: *Crassiora*; the solid, tangible components of a body as opposed to its volatile spirits.</note>.

EXPLA- <note>The text breaks mid-word; it is the beginning of *Explicatio*, or "Explanation."</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that the evaporation of spirits is a mutual attraction between the spirit and the air, and introduces a new rule stating that trapped spirits will soften the solid matter they inhabit if they cannot create more of themselves.</summary>

<keywords>evaporation, spirits, atmospheric attraction, appetite of air, matter, softening, Francis Bacon, natural philosophy, Rule VIII</keywords>

### EXPLANATION.

The generation of a new spirit does not occur except upon those substances which are in a degree closer to the nature of a spirit; such as moist things <note>original: *Humida*; referring to fluids or vapors that are easily converted into spirit.</note>. Therefore, if the coarser parts <note>original: *Partes Crassiores*; the solid, tangible components of a body.</note> (among which the spirit is engaged) are in a more remote degree, although the spirit cannot fully transform <note>original: *conficere*; in Bacon's natural philosophy, this refers to the spirit "digesting" or converting matter into its own substance.</note> them into new spirit, it nevertheless—insofar as it is able—weakens, softens, and dissolves them. The result is that while the spirit cannot increase its own quantity <note>original: *Quantum*; the total volume or mass of the spirit.</note>, it may nonetheless dwell more loosely <note>original: *laxius*; meaning with more space or less constriction within the solid structure.</note> and live among those things which are more friendly <note>original: *Amica*; substances that are more compatible with or "kindred" to the spirit's nature.</note> to it. Moreover, this aphorism is exceedingly useful for our purpose; because it points toward the <term>inteneration|The process of making something tender or soft; a key goal in Bacon's quest for longevity, as it counters the hardening of the body that comes with age.</term> of stubborn parts through the detention <note>original: *Detentionem*; the act of keeping the spirit trapped within the body rather than letting it evaporate into the air.</note> of the spirit.

### RULE IX.

<summary>Bacon explains that when spirits are trapped within a body, they work to soften and dissolve the surrounding solid matter to create a more hospitable environment for themselves, even if they cannot convert that matter into more spirit. This process of "inteneration" is presented as a vital method for maintaining the body's youthfulness.</summary>

<keywords>spirit, moisture, matter, inteneration, detention of spirits, transformation, softening, Francis Bacon, natural philosophy</keywords>

->RULE IX.<-

**T**he <term>inteneration|The process of making the body's tissues tender or soft; Bacon viewed aging as a hardening and drying of the body, so 'inteneration' is the key to rejuvenation.</term> of the harder parts proceeds well when the spirit neither evaporates nor generates <note>original: *generet*; here meaning the spirit creating more of itself by consuming the body's fluids.</note>.

->EXPLANATION.<-

### EXPLANATION.

&nbsp;

In inanimate things, this Rule holds true with certainty; for inequality is the mother of dissolution <note>original: *Dissolutionis Mater*; Bacon suggests that unevenness or lack of symmetry in a substance’s internal structure leads to its physical breakdown.</note>. However, in animate beings—because we must consider not only consumption but also repair, and repair proceeds through the appetites <note>original: *Appetitus*. In Baconian physics, "appetites" are the natural inclinations or drives of matter to seek out what is necessary for its own restoration or preservation.</note> of things, which are in turn sharpened by variety—this rule does not hold quite so rigidly. Yet it should be accepted to this extent: that this variety should be an alternation rather than a confusion, and remain, as it were, "constant in its inconstancy."

&nbsp;

->**The Spirit** within a body of<-
->**solid structure** is<-
->**held fast**, though<-
->**against its will**.<-

<meta>This page provides the explanation for Rule XV, which was introduced on the previous page. It discusses the physical constraints that govern how spirits and other substances move through the pores and passages of solid matter.</meta>

*History of Life and Death.* 431

&nbsp;

### EXPLANATION.

&nbsp;

All things abhor a <term>solution of continuity</term> <note>original: *solutionem continuitatis*; a term from natural philosophy and medicine referring to a physical break, gap, or separation in an otherwise continuous substance.</note>; yet they do so according to the degree of their density or thinness. For indeed, the more subtle or thin bodies are, the more they allow themselves to be compressed into smaller and narrower <term>passages</term> <note>original: *meatus*; the internal channels or pathways within a material structure.</note>. Thus, water will enter a passage that dust will not; air will enter where water will not; even flame and spirit will enter where air will not.

Nevertheless, there is a certain limit to this; for the spirit does not labor with such a great "desire to escape" <note>original: *desiderio exeundi*; Bacon frequently describes the "spirit" of a substance as having a natural impulse to flee its material confinement and return to the air.</note> that it permits itself to be broken apart too much, or forced into excessively tight pores or passages. Therefore, if the spirit is enclosed by a body that is hard, or even oily and tenacious, (which... <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "quod" (which), indicating the explanation continues on the following page.</note>

&nbsp;

<summary>Bacon explains that while spirits are highly fluid and can penetrate small openings, they still resist being fragmented. This resistance allows dense or "tenacious" materials to successfully trap the spirit and prevent its escape.</summary>
<keywords>solution of continuity, density, meatus, passages, spirit, desire to escape, material structure, porosity, Baconian physics</keywords>

(which is not easily divided) <note>original: *quod non facile diuiditur*; continuing the sentence from the previous page describing a body that is hard or oily.</note> is surrounded, it is plainly constrained, and as if imprisoned, and it sets aside its <term>appetite for departing</term> <note>original: *Appetitum exeundi*; the natural tendency of the internal spirit to escape the body and join the surrounding air, which Bacon identifies as a primary cause of aging and decay.</note>; For this reason we see that Metals and Stones require a long age before the Spirit departs; unless either the Spirit is excited by Fire, or the thicker parts are separated by <term>corrosive and strong waters</term> <note>original: *Aquis Corrodentibus, & Fortibus*; a reference to acids used in alchemy and early chemistry, such as *aqua fortis* (nitric acid), which break down solid matter.</note>. Similar is the logic for <term>tenacious</term> <note>original: *Tenaciũ*; substances that are sticky, viscous, or hold together firmly.</note> substances, such as Gums, except that they are dissolved by a gentler heat. Therefore, the hard juices of the body, constricted skin, and similar things (which are produced by dryness of nourishment, exercise, and the coldness of the air) are useful for longevity; because they surround the spirit with tight enclosures, so that it cannot escape.

<summary>Bacon argues that materials with high density or stickiness effectively imprison the spirit, preventing it from escaping. He suggests that for humans, things like firm skin and a dry diet can help retain the vital spirit and extend life.</summary>

RULE XVI.

In **Oily** and **Fatty** substances, the **Spirit** is detained willingly, even if they are not **Tenacious**.

EXPLANATION.

The **Spirit**, if it is neither provoked by the <term>antipathy</term> <note>original: *Antipathiâ*; in early modern science, "antipathy" referred to a natural repulsion between two substances that cannot coexist peacefully.</note> of the surrounding body, nor nourished by too great a likeness to that body, nor drawn out or incited by an external body, does not struggle much to **Exit**. All these conditions are met in oily substances; for they are not as hostile to the Spirit as hard substances are; nor are they as similar to the spirit as <term>watery substances</term> <note>original: *Aquea*; Bacon believed the spirit was "watery" in nature, and thus more likely to leave a body to join with other watery or airy elements.</note> are; nor do they harmonize well with the surrounding **Air**.

[space]

CANON <note>The catchword "CANON" (Rule) indicates that the text of the next rule begins on the following page.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that oily and fatty substances preserve the "spirit" of a body not by being physically hard, but because the spirit feels no urge to escape them or merge with the surrounding air.</summary>
<keywords>spirit, oiliness, fat, antipathy, evaporation, preservation, moisture, Francis Bacon</keywords>

We have stated that **watery substances**, being <term>consubstantial</term> <note>original: *Consubstantialia*; Bacon uses this to mean substances that share the same fundamental essence or "matter," which in his theory of physics makes them more likely to blend or dissolve into one another.</note> with the **Air**, evaporate more quickly; **oily substances** evaporate more slowly, as they are less in <term>agreement</term> <note>original: *consentientia*; in early modern science, "agreement" or "sympathy" referred to the natural inclination of certain substances to harmonize or move toward one another.</note> with the Air. # RULE XVIII.

We stated a little while ago that the **Escape of the Spirit** is a **twofold action**, arising from the **Appetite** <term>Appetite</term> <note>original: *Appetitu*; in the physics of the 1600s, "appetite" was used to describe the natural tendency, "desire," or attraction that one substance has for another.</note> of both the **Spirit** and the **Air**. Therefore, if one [of these two forces is removed]...

<summary>Bacon concludes that gentle exercise preserves the body's vital oils and maintains youthfulness. He then introduces the idea that blocking the air's "appetite" for the body's internal spirits can help prevent them from escaping, thus extending life.</summary>

# RULE XIX.

The **Nature of the Spirits** is like the **supreme Wheel**, which drives the other **Wheels** in the Human Body. <note>Bacon uses a mechanical metaphor here: the "spirits" are the primary engine or master gear of the biological machine. ...of **Longevity**, this [intention] should be placed first.

To this is added, that a more easy and direct **Path** lies open to altering the **Spirits** than to other parts of the body. For there is a **twofold Operation** upon the spirits: one through **Nourishment** <note>original: *alimenta*; meaning food and drink processed through the long "circuit" of digestion.</note>, which is slow and occurs, as it were, by a circuitous route; the other (which is itself twofold) is **sudden** and aims directly at the spirits. These are namely through **Vapors**, or through the **Emotions**. <note>original: *affectus*; Bacon refers to the passions or states of mind, which he believed could instantaneously expand or contract the vital spirits.</note>

# RULE XX.

->**Body Juices** that are somewhat **Hard** and **Dewy** contribute to **Longevity**.<-

### EXPLANATION.

The **Reason** is clear, since we have previously established that substances which are **Hard** and **Oily** (or **Dewy**) are dissipated <note>In Bacon's theory, aging is caused by the "predatory" vital spirit consuming the body's moisture; if the moisture is "hard" or "oily," it resists being evaporated or consumed.</note> with greater difficulty.

<note>A faint circular mark, possibly bleed-through from the reverse side of the page, is visible behind the "Rule XX" and "Explanation" sections.</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that the quickest way to restore youth is by acting directly on the vital spirits via vapors or emotions. He then explains that a body's internal moisture must be "hard" and "dewy" to resist the natural decay caused by evaporation.</summary>

<keywords>vital spirits, vapors, passions of the mind, Rule XX, bodily juices, moisture, dissipation, Francis Bacon</keywords>

...dissipated. ### EXPLANATION.

This **Rule** is more difficult in **Practice** than in **Understanding**: For it is manifest that whatever penetrates well, but does so with a **Sting** or a **Tooth** <note>original: *Dente*; Bacon uses this metaphor to describe the "biting" or corrosive quality of certain sharp, acidic substances.</note> (as do all **Sharp** and **Acidic** things), leaves behind wherever it passes a certain trace of **Dryness** and **Tearing** <note>original: *Diuulsionis*; a pulling apart or rending of the body's delicate fibers.</note>; such that it hardens the **Juices** and convulses the **Parts** of the body. But on the contrary, those substances which penetrate by **Mere Thinness**, as if stealthily and by insinuation, without violence, **Moisten** and **Water** the body in their passage. Regarding these, we have described not a few things in the **Fourth** and **Seventh Operations** <note>Bacon refers back to his specific medicinal "operations": the fourth focused on "extinguishing" the spirit and the seventh on "moistening the parts."</note>.

# RULE XXIII.

**Nourishment from the outside, or at least not through the stomach, is most beneficial for Longevity, if it can be achieved.**

### EXPLANATION.

We see that all things performed through **Nutrition** occur by long and winding paths <note>original: *Ambages*; referring to the complex, indirect process of digestion where food must be broken down, sorted, and transported through various organs.</note>; whereas those things which happen through the **"embrace" of like substances** <note>original: *Amplexus similium*; Bacon’s theory that similar substances naturally attract and bond with one another without the need for complex mechanical processing.</note> (as occurs in **Infusions**) require no long delay. Therefore, **Nourishment from the outside** would be most useful, and all the more so,

<summary>Bacon concludes his thoughts on Rule XXII and introduces Rule XXIII, arguing that because digestion is a slow and indirect process, finding ways to nourish the body through the skin or other external means would significantly extend life.</summary>

# RULE XXV.

**Every sudden renovation of the body occurs either through the Spirits or through Softening.** <note>original: *Malacissationes*; a technical term in early medicine for the softening of dry or hardened tissues using baths, oils, or poultices.</note>

### EXPLANATION.

There are two primary components in the body: the **Spirits** and the **Parts** <note>Bacon distinguishes between the "spirits" (the active, pneumatic essence that governs life) and the "parts" (the solid, tangible tissues and organs).</note>. One reaches both of these through the long path of **Nutrition**. However, there are "short paths" to these components: the **Spirits** are reached through **Vapors** and **Emotions**, and the **Parts** are reached through **Softening** <term>Malacissations<gloss>The use of external treatments to restore flexibility to the body’s dry, aging fibers.</gloss></term>. 

Yet this must be noted a bit more carefully: we in no way confuse **Nourish-**

<summary>Bacon concludes that external treatments can assist failing internal digestion. He then introduces Rule XXV, asserting that the body can be quickly renewed either by influencing the vital spirits (via vapors/emotions) or by softening the solid tissues (via external applications).</summary>

# RULE XXVI.

**Softening is achieved through substances that share the same substance, those that penetrate, and those that seal.** <note>original: *Consubstantialia, Imprimentia, & Occludentia*</note>

### EXPLANATION.

The reason for this is clear: substances that are **Consubstantial** <term>of the same substance<gloss>Materials that are physically or chemically similar to the body's tissues, making them more compatible for absorption.</gloss></term> are what truly perform the softening; those that **Penetrate** lead the treatment deep into the tissues; and those that **Seal** <term>Occluding<gloss>Substances like oils or waxes that block the pores to prevent the escape of moisture or spirits.</gloss></term> retain the treatment and check **Perspiration**, which is a process directly opposed to **Softening**. Therefore (as discussed in the Ninth Operation... <note>The text breaks off here; Bacon is referring to his "Ten Operations" for longevity found earlier in the treatise.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that "softening" the body is not about feeding it, but preparing the tissues to better absorb nutrients. He identifies three essential components for this process: compatible materials, penetrating agents, and sealants to prevent moisture loss.</summary>
<keywords>softening, malacissation, nutrition, consubstantial substances, penetration, perspiration, Rule XXVI</keywords>

[...as we have described] **Softening** cannot be done well all at once, but through a specific **Sequence** and **Order**: First, by excluding external liquid through **Thickening agents** <note>original: *Spissamenta*; substances used to condense the body's surface or close off moisture.</note>; because a foreign and thick infusion does not unify well with the **Body**. That which enters must be subtle, and of the nature of a **Vapor**. Second, by **Softening** <note>original: *Intenerando*; to make tender or supple.</note> through the affinity of **Consubstantial** materials <term>Consubstantial<gloss>Substances that share a similar physical nature or "essence" with the human body, making them easier for the tissues to accept.</gloss></term>: For bodies, at the touch of those things with which they have a strong affinity, open themselves and relax their **Pores**. Third, the **Penetrating agents** act as vehicles and press in some of the **Consubstantial** materials; meanwhile, a mixture of mild **Astringents** <note>original: *Astringentium*; substances that contract body tissues.</note> slightly restrains **Perspiration**. But there follows in the fourth place that **Great Binding** and **Closing** through the use of **Plasters** <note>original: *Emplastrationem*; the application of thick, medicinal pastes or poultices.</note>; and afterwards gradually through **Anointing**, until the softened state is converted...

<summary>Bacon outlines a four-step medical sequence for "softening" the body: excluding coarse liquids, opening pores via substances with natural affinity, using vehicles to drive the treatment deep while checking perspiration, and finally sealing the skin with plasters and oils.</summary>

### RULE XXVII.
The frequent **Renewal** of things that are **Repairable** also refreshes those things that are **Less Repairable**.

### EXPLANATION.
We stated in the very **Introduction** <note>original: *Aditu ipso*; literally the "entrance" or opening portal of the text.</note> of this History that the **Way of Death** is this: that the more **Repairable** parts perish in the company of those that are **Less Repairable**; thus, we must labor with all our might in the **Restoration** of these kinds of less repairable parts. Therefore, prompted by **Aristotle’s Observation** regarding **Plants**—namely, that the **Newness** of the **Branches** <note>Aristotle noted that the fresh growth of new shoots helps sustain the vitality of the main trunk.</note> refreshes the **Trunk** itself as the life-force passes through it—we have concluded that a similar **Logic** would hold true if frequently

### RULE XXIX.

That **Complication**, namely that both **Consumption** and **Repair** are the **Works of Heat**, is the greatest **Obstacle** to **Longevity**.

### EXPLANATION.

Almost all **Great Works** are destroyed by **Complicated Natures** <note>original: *Naturis Complicatis*; Bacon refers to complex systems where a single force produces opposing results.</note>; since what helps in one **Respect**, may hurt in another; And here there is need for **Balanced Judgment**, and **Sagacious Practice**: This we, as much as the matter permits, and for the present... <note>Bacon is setting the stage to explain how to manage the "heat" of the body so it repairs the flesh without "consuming" or drying out the vital spirits.</note>

<summary>Bacon concludes that internal cooling is limited by the need to protect the stomach's digestive heat, then introduces Rule XXIX: the paradox that heat both sustains life through repair and shortens it through consumption.</summary>

### RULE XXX.

The **Cure of Diseases** requires **Temporary Medicines**; but **Longevity of Life** must be expected from **Regimens** <note>original: *Diætis*. ### EXPLANATION.

Those things that occur by **Accident** <note>original: *ex Accidente*; refers to external or incidental causes, such as an infection or an injury, rather than the natural, internal process of aging.</note> cease once their **Causes** are removed. But the **Continuous Course of Nature**, like a **Flowing River**, requires continuous **Rowing** or **Sailing** against the current <note>Bacon uses this vivid metaphor to explain that aging is a constant, natural force; unlike a temporary illness that can be "cured," the passage of time must be actively resisted every day.</note>. Therefore, one must proceed regularly through **Regimens**. Regimens, moreover, are of two kinds;

CANON XXXI.

**T**he living spirit suffers **Destruction** immediately when it is deprived of either **Motion**, **Cooling**, or **Nourishment**.

EXPLANATION.

**T**hese are, of course, those three things which we previously called the **Small Porches of Death** <note>original: *Atriola Mortis*. Bacon uses this architectural metaphor to describe the primary thresholds or "entryways" through which death overtakes the body's vital functions.</note>; and they are the afflictions specific and immediate to the **Spirit**. For indeed, all the organs of the **Principal Parts** <note>In the medical tradition of the time, these were the heart, brain, lungs, and liver, which were thought to generate and govern the vital and natural spirits.</note> serve to ensure that these three duties are performed. And again, every destruction of the organs that is lethal leads to this: that one or more of these three functions fail. Therefore, all other things are various <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, indicating that the list of "various" secondary causes of death continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>Bacon defines the three immediate causes of death—lack of motion, lack of cooling, and lack of nourishment—which he calls the "Small Porches of Death." He explains that all vital organs exist primarily to support these three functions of the vital spirit.</summary>
<keywords>vital spirit, Porches of Death, motion, refrigeration, nourishment, principal organs, causes of death, Francis Bacon</keywords>

...various paths to Death; but they end in these <note>original: *viæ ad Mortem*. This continues from the previous page's discussion on how all secondary causes of death eventually lead to the failure of the three primary functions: motion, cooling, and nourishment.</note>. Now the structure of the Parts <note>The physical anatomy of the body.</note> is the Organ of the Spirit; just as the Spirit itself is the organ of the rational Soul; which is Incorporeal, and Divine.

CANON XXXII.

**T**he substance of **F**lame is momentary; that of Air is fixed; the nature <note>original: *Ratio*. Here meaning the essential character or proportional middle-ground.</note> of the Living Spirit in animals is a middle state between them.

EXPLANATION.

**T**his is a matter requiring both deeper investigation and a longer explanation than serves our present inquiry <note>Bacon often defers highly theoretical physics or metaphysics to other works, focusing here on the practical "history" or data of life and death.</note>. In the meantime, it must be understood that Flame is continually being generated and extinguished, so that it persists only by succession <note>Much like a river appears constant but is made of new water, Bacon views fire as a process of constant renewal rather than a stable "thing."</note>...

<summary>Bacon explains that the physical body serves the spirit, which in turn serves the divine soul. He then defines the vital spirit as a substance existing halfway between the fleeting nature of fire and the permanent nature of air.</summary>
<keywords>rational soul, vital spirit, flame, air, hierarchy of soul, physiology, Francis Bacon, nature of spirit</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Canon XXXII, detailing the difference between the fleeting nature of flame and the stability of air, then defining the vital spirit as a hybrid of both.</meta>

**History of Life & Death.** [Page number] **453**

...is only continued by succession <note>original: *successionem tantùm continuetur*. This completes the thought from the previous page that flame is a process of constant renewal rather than a permanent object.</note>. But Air is a fixed Body, and is not dissolved; for although Air may generate New Air from Watery Moisture, nevertheless the old Air remains nonetheless; from which arises that over-burdening <note>original: *super-oneratio*. The idea that the atmosphere accumulates rather than dissipates.</note> of the Air, of which we spoke in the Section on Winds <note>original: *Titulo de Ventis*. Referring to Bacon's work "Historia Ventorum" (History of the Winds).</note>. But the Spirit partakes of both Natures, both of Flame and of Air; just as its fuels <note>original: *fomites*. Literally "kindling" or "tinder," referring to the substances that sustain it.</note> are Oil, which is of the same nature as Flame, and Air <note>The Latin text notes air is homogeneous with water, reflecting the theory that air and water are part of the same "watery" family of elements.</note>, which is of the same nature as Water. For the Spirit is not nourished by the Oily substance alone, nor by the Watery substance alone, but by both; and although neither Air with Flame, nor Oil with Water combine well together, they nevertheless agree well enough in a Mixture. Furthermore, the Spirit receives from Air its easy and delicate im-
                                             pressions <note>original: *impref-* (likely *impressiones*). This refers to how the spirit can be molded by sensations or movements due to its airy quality.</note>

<summary>Bacon contrasts the stable nature of air with the fleeting nature of fire, concluding that the "vital spirit" in living beings is a unique mixture that draws nourishment from both oily and watery substances.</summary>

454 | *History of Life & Death.*

&nbsp; | pressions <note>original: *preffiones*. This completes the word "impressions" from the bottom of page 453.</note> and Receptions; 
&nbsp; | from Flame, however, it derives its Noble and 
&nbsp; | Powerful Motions and Activities.
&nbsp; | Similarly, the Duration of the Spirit 
&nbsp; | is a composite thing; it is neither as Momen-
&nbsp; | tary as Flame, nor yet 
&nbsp; | as fixed as Air; And for this reason, 
&nbsp; | it especially does not follow the nature of Flame, 
&nbsp; | because Flame itself is also extinguished 
&nbsp; | by Accident <note>original: *per Accidens*. Bacon refers to external circumstances or "contrary forces" that quench a fire, rather than its own internal exhaustion.</note>; namely, by the Contraries 
&nbsp; | and Destructive forces surrounding it; 
&nbsp; | a cause and necessity which 
&nbsp; | the Spirit does not share in the same way. 
&nbsp; | Moreover, the Spirit is Repaired 
&nbsp; | from the lively and Florid Blood 
&nbsp; | of the small Arteries <note>original: *exilium* (from *exilis*), meaning thin or slender vessels.</note> that wind 
&nbsp; | into the Brain; but 
&nbsp; | this Repair happens in its own way, of which 
&nbsp; | we are not speaking at this time.
&nbsp; | ->**THE END.**<-
&nbsp; | <note>An oval library stamp reads: Royal Library of Munich (Bibliotheca Regia Monacensis).</note>

<summary>Bacon concludes his treatise by defining the vital spirit as a hybrid substance that possesses the active power of flame and the receptivity of air, sustained by the blood within the brain. This final page emphasizes that unlike fire, which is easily extinguished by external forces, the spirit is a more stable, self-regulating composite.</summary>
<keywords>vital spirit, physiology, brain, arteries, duration, flame, air, Francis Bacon, soul and body</keywords>
